⏺ I read the client, consumer, server config and deployment/config.md. Here's
  the plan, built around what the code supports today.

  Fix before testing

  These would make the results wrong or unusable:

  1. Only 2 targets are registered. config.md lists only Servers 1 and 2 under
     "Target Group Members". It also labels two instances "Server 3"; the second
     is i-013f48cd38e7e5afa. For the 1-instance and 2-instance runs, deregister
     the extra instances. Don't just stop them, because the ALB would still
     route to them until their health checks fail.
  2. The consumer is capped at 10 threads. ConsumerConfig clamps
     CONSUMER_MAX_THREADS to the number of resort queues (10), and every queue
     has x-single-active-consumer. Setting 20, 40 or 80 would still give you 10
     threads. You have two options:
     - (a) Test 10 threads, plus 1, 2 and 5 below it, and explain the cap in the
       report: per-resort ordering requires one consumer per queue.
     - (b) Split the work into more queues, for example resort.N.shard.K.

     I recommend (a). It's a defensible design choice and needs no code changes.
     Also turn off the autoscaler (CONSUMER_AUTOSCALE=false) during tuning
     runs, or the thread count will change mid-run.
  3. The queue limits can drop swipes silently. Each queue has
     x-max-length=10000 (overflow drops the oldest message) and
     x-message-ttl=60s. Both send dropped messages to skitrak.dlx, so the DLQ
     message count is your "swipe loss" metric. Record it after every run. Any
     non-zero count fails the "no swipe loss" target.
  4. Nothing counts processed swipes end to end. The client counts successful
     POSTs. The consumer has duplicate, retry and dead-letter counters but no
     "processed" total, so you can't prove "client successes = consumer
     applied". I'd add a processed counter to the consumer's /health details.
     It's a small change. Alternatively, you can use the RabbitMQ "deliver/ack"
     totals from the management API.
  5. Run the client on an EC2 instance in us-west-2, not your laptop. Otherwise
     WAN latency becomes the bottleneck and hides any gain from adding servers.
  6. Watch t3.micro CPU credits. A 1M-swipe run can use up the burst credits,
     and throughput then falls off a cliff that looks like a bottleneck in your
     code. Either enable "unlimited" mode on the instances or record
     CPUCreditBalance and wait for it to recover between runs.

  Metrics for each run

  Source: Client
  What: runtime, throughput, failures, retries, p50/p95/p99 latency, updates
  received per subscriber, push latency
  How: already printed by LoadTestClient; save stdout plus results/ for each run
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Source: RabbitMQ
  What: queue depth (peak and average), publish rate, deliver/ack rate, DLQ
  count
  How: a script polling GET :15672/api/queues every 2s into a CSV; also
  screenshot the management UI graphs
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Source: ALB
  What: RequestCountPerTarget, TargetResponseTime, HTTPCode_Target_5XX,
  HealthyHostCount
  How: CloudWatch
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Source: EC2
  What: CPU, network in/out, CPU credits
  How: CloudWatch; memory needs the agent, or run free -m / vmstat 5 over SSH
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Source: Consumer
  What: consumer lag, thread count, dead-letter and retry counts
  How: consumer /health on port 8081, polled during the run

  I can write the RabbitMQ and consumer polling script plus a small plotting
  script. The queue-depth plot is how you show the "plateau vs sawtooth"
  profile.

  Test matrix (in order)

  Phase A: tuning, 1 server, 200K swipes each run so it stays fast
  1. Client threads 64, 128, 256, 512 (args[1]) at fixed consumer settings →
     find the knee in throughput and latency.
  2. At the best client thread count: consumer threads 1, 2, 5, 10, then
     prefetch 10, 50, 100, 250 → aim for queue depth under 1000 and a plateaued
     profile.
  3. Server RABBITMQ_CHANNEL_POOL_SIZE at 16, 64, 128, and publisher confirms on
     vs off (report the safety trade-off if you turn them off).

  The assignment also lists batch ack size. The consumer acks one message at a
  time today, so either note that or add basicAck(tag, multiple=true) every N
  messages.

  Phase B: required runs, using the tuned settings

  ┌─────┬─────────┬───────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Run │ Targets │                Swipes                 │
  ├─────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ B1  │ 1       │ 500K                                  │
  ├─────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ B2  │ 2       │ 500K                                  │
  ├─────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ B3  │ 4       │ 500K                                  │
  ├─────┼─────────┼───────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ B4  │ 4       │ 1M (stress test; find the bottleneck) │
  └─────┴─────────┴───────────────────────────────────────┘

  The client's total includes the 32K warmup, so pass 532000 and 1032000. Run
  each one at least twice. For B4, the likely bottleneck is the single RabbitMQ
  or consumer t3.micro rather than the ingest tier. The CPU charts will show
  which.

  Phase C: ALB behaviour, short runs on 4 targets
  - Load distribution: compare RequestCountPerTarget across the 4 targets.
    Java's HttpClient doesn't store cookies, so ingest POSTs should spread
    across all targets. Note that in the report.
  - WebSocket stickiness: each of the 10 subscribers stays on the server that
    accepted it. Show this through the per-server connection logs or the
    serverId in leaderboard messages, plus a "Reconnects = 0" column. Because
    the leaderboard goes through the fanout backplane, subscribers on any server
    still receive every resort's updates.
  - Failover: start a 500K run, then stop Tomcat on one server about 30 seconds
    in. Record the failure and retry spike, the time until the ALB marks it
    unhealthy (about 90s with 3 failures at 30s intervals), whether subscribers
    on that server reconnect to another one, and whether the client's retries
    absorb the outage. Then restart Tomcat and time how long it takes to become
    healthy again (about 60s).
  - Latency impact: compare p50/p99 for a direct :8080 hit on one server against
    the same server through the ALB.

  Correctness check (every Phase B run): the client's update count is above 0
  for every resort. Check that the final leaderboard snapshot matches the swipes
  sent, that the DLQ count is 0, and that consumer processed equals client
  successes minus duplicates.

  Small client changes worth making

  - Make warmup optional or configurable, so the 1M stress test is exactly 1M.
  - Write results to results/<runLabel>/ so runs don't overwrite each other.
    Today every run writes to the same results/ path.

❯ why don't we just use bash scripts?                                           

⏺ We can. Nothing here needs Python. I chose it for three reasons:

  1. Arithmetic. The queue monitor computes rates, sums over 10+ queues, and lag
     as depth ÷ ack rate. Bash has no floating point, so every one of those
     calculations goes through awk or bc.
  2. JSON. The RabbitMQ API and the consumer /health both return JSON. In bash
     you need jq, which isn't installed by default on Amazon Linux 2023 (sudo 
     dnf install jq fixes that). Python's JSON support is built in.
  3. Dependencies. Python 3 comes preinstalled on Amazon Linux 2023, so the
     scripts run on the EC2 boxes with no setup.

  Bash does fit some of this better:

  - Host metrics. vmstat -n 2 already reports CPU, including steal (st), and
    memory. A short awk over /proc/net/dev covers the network. That's simpler
    than my host_monitor.py.
  - Orchestration. Starting monitors over SSH, running the client jar, and
    collecting files back is shell work anyway.
  - CloudWatch. aws cloudwatch get-metric-data plus jq works fine in bash.

  The one piece I'd still keep in Python is the final step that combines the
  client log and the CSVs into a single summary row, and draws the queue-depth
  plot. That step does parsing and statistics (averages, peaks, percentiles),
  which is painful in awk.

  My recommendation is to write the monitors and the run script in bash with
  curl + jq + vmstat + awk, and keep Python only for summarizing and plotting.
  If you'd rather avoid Python entirely, the summary can also be done in awk;
  it's just harder to read.

  I've written three Python files so far: monitoring/queue_monitor.py,
  monitoring/host_monitor.py and monitoring/cloudwatch_fetch.py. Should I
  rewrite them as bash and delete the Python versions?

⏺ The monitoring scripts are written and tested locally against fake services,
  but not yet against your AWS setup. host_monitor.py hasn't run at all, because
  it needs Linux and Docker isn't running here.

  You start a test with run_test.py. It records starting counts, starts the
  monitors, runs the client jar, and waits for the queues to drain after the
  client finishes. It then saves everything to results/<label>/ and adds one row
  per run to results/summary.csv.

  What each run collects
  - Client: runtime, throughput, failures, retries, p50/p95/p99 latency, and
    leaderboard updates per subscriber (to show that subscribers really receive
    updates). These are parsed from the client's own output, so the client code
    is unchanged.
  - Queue: depth every 2 seconds (peak, average, p95, and % of samples
    over 1000), producer rate, consumer rate, how long the queue takes to drain,
    and a count of how often the depth rose and fell. That count separates a
    stable plateau (0–1) from a sawtooth (several).
  - Consumer lag: estimated as queue depth ÷ ack rate. Nothing in the system
    measures lag directly, so this is the closest available figure for the "<
    100 ms" target.
  - Swipe loss: compares the client's successful swipes with how many the
    consumer actually processed during the run. A run passes only if none are
    missing, none went to the dead-letter queue, and the queues emptied.
  - System: CPU on every host (including steal, which shows a t3 instance
    running out of CPU credits), memory, Java memory, network and TCP
    connections. CloudWatch adds ALB and EC2 metrics.
  - Requests per ingest server: CloudWatch doesn't split ALB requests by
    instance, so these come from each server's Tomcat access log.

  Local tests (fake services)
  - queue_monitor.py against a fake RabbitMQ API and consumer /health: the CSV
    and rates came out right, and it shut down cleanly.
  - summarize.py on a sample run whose log matches the client's print format
    exactly: every field parsed.
  - run_test.py end to end with a stand-in client jar: everything ran. It
    reported "not drained / FAIL" because the fake queue never empties, which is
    the correct result.
  - Not tested locally: the SSH steps, the access-log counts, and the CloudWatch
    calls. Your first real run will exercise those.

  I also fixed a bug in cloudwatch_fetch.py: it built the wrong target-group
  name for CloudWatch, so those queries would have returned no data.

  Before the first run
  1. Copy test_config.example.json to test_config.json and fill in your SSH key
     and RabbitMQ credentials.
  2. Run the scripts from a machine that can reach RabbitMQ on port 15672, the
     consumer on port 8081, and every host over SSH. Your current security
     groups only cover port 8080, so add those rules.
  3. CloudWatch reports 1–2 minutes late. A few minutes after each run, rerun
     the command saved in cloudwatch_cmd.txt.

  A good first step is a short smoke test through one server, for example
  --swipes 50000 --ingest server1, to check the SSH and access-log steps before
  the long runs.

  Files are in monitoring/:
  - run_test.py
  - queue_monitor.py
  - host_monitor.py
  - cloudwatch_fetch.py
  - summarize.py
  - test_config.example.json
  - README.md

❯ The current file structure looks reasonable. Please keep it as is and avoid   
  adding unnecessary files or features. Make sure each script has a clear       
  responsibility, avoid duplicate metric collection, and keep the               
  implementation focused strictly on Part 4 requirements.                       

⏺ I'll check each script for overlap against the Part 4 list (client runtime,
  throughput, failures and retries; queue depth and producer and consumer rates;
  CPU, memory and network; queue depth, lag and swipe-loss targets; subscribers
  receiving updates). There is real duplication:

  - CPU and network are collected twice: by host_monitor.py and by the EC2
    metrics in cloudwatch_fetch.py.
  - Consumer rate is collected three times: RabbitMQ deliver rate, RabbitMQ ack
    rate, and the consumer's processed counter.
  - Consumer counters are sampled every 2s by queue_monitor.py and also
    snapshotted by run_test.py.
  - Extras outside Part 4: relay-queue depth, subscriber drops/reconnects, push
    latency, load/iowait/TCP/Java memory, and ALB connection counts.

  The plan, one job per script:
  - queue_monitor.py: RabbitMQ only. Depth, producer rate (publish), consumer
    rate (ack), and lag estimated from those.
  - host_monitor.py: CPU, memory and network only.
  - cloudwatch_fetch.py: only what the other scripts can't see, meaning ALB-side
    errors, healthy targets, and server-side response time. EC2 metrics are
    removed.
  - run_test.py: the only source of before/after consumer and dead-letter
    counters, used for the swipe-loss check.

      47 +`est_drain_*_ms` is queue depth ÷ ack rate, an **estimate** of 
         +how long the current backlog takes to drain. It is not measured
         + enqueue-to-consumption latency, so the summary gives no pass/f

      48

         -lures, retries, latency percentiles, leaderboard updates per su
         -bscriber, drops, reconnects, push latency.                     
      45 -- **Queue:** peak, average, and p95 depth; percentage of sample
         -s above 1000; `queue_depth_cycles` (0–1 is a plateau, several i
         -s a sawtooth); producer rate (publish) and consumer rate (from 
         -the consumer's processed counter); drain time.                 
      46 -- **Consumer lag:** `est_lag_*_ms`, estimated as queue depth ÷ 
         -ack rate (how long a newly published swipe waits). It is an est
         -imate. For true end-to-end latency, use the client's push laten
         -cy.                                                            
      47 -- **Swipe loss:** `swipes_missing` = client successes − swipes 
         -the consumer processed during the run. `loss_check` is PASS onl
         -y if nothing is missing, nothing went to the DLQ, and the queue
         -s drained. Consumer counters reset if the consumer restarts mid
         --run; the summary flags that case.                             
      48 -- **System:** for each host, average and max CPU, max steal (a 
         -sign that a t3 instance has used up its CPU credits), max memor
         -y, Java RSS, and average network.                              
      49 -- **Distribution:** POST requests per ingest server and each se
         -rver's share.                                                  
      49 +Each swipe event already carries `timestamp`, the ingest server
         +'s receive time (`SwipeEvent.timestamp`, set in `SkierServlet`)
         +. The consumer parses it but does not record `processing time −
         + timestamp`. Measuring real lag needs that one addition in the 
         +consumer, exposed on `/health`. The client's "push latency" doe
         +s not substitute for it: it measures consumer broadcast → clien
         +t, not enqueue → consume.                                      

  Listed 1 directory (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ I've simplified the monitoring scripts. Each metric now comes from one place,
  and the local smoke test passes. It ran against fake services, so the SSH
  steps, the access-log counts and the CloudWatch calls haven't run against your
  AWS setup yet.

  Consumer lag can't be measured yet. Every swipe event already carries
  timestamp: the time the ingest server received it (set in
  SkierServlet.java:117). The consumer reads it but never compares it with the
  time it processes the swipe, so the scripts have no real lag to collect. The
  client's "push latency" isn't a substitute: it measures consumer → client, not
  queue → consumer.

  So the summary reports only est_drain_*_ms (queue depth ÷ ack rate), labeled
  everywhere as an estimate of how long the backlog takes to drain. I removed
  the lag pass/fail check, and nothing claims the < 100 ms target is met.
  Measuring it properly needs one consumer change: record processing time − 
  event.timestamp and report percentiles on /health. Say if you want it; it's a
  code change, so I haven't made it.

  What changed
  - queue_monitor.py: RabbitMQ only, writing depth, producer rate, consumer rate
    and the drain estimate. The consumer /health polling and the extra queue
    fields are gone.
  - host_monitor.py: CPU (with steal), memory and network only.
  - cloudwatch_fetch.py: only what the ALB alone can see: ALB-generated and
    server-generated 5XX errors, healthy target count, and server-side response
    time. The EC2 CPU and network metrics duplicated host_monitor.py, so they're
    removed.
  - run_test.py: the only place that reads the consumer's counters, before and
    after each run, for the swipe-loss check.
  - summarize.py: only Part 4 fields. Subscriber drops and reconnects and push
    latency are removed; the leaderboard update count is kept to show
    subscribers get real updates.
  - test_config.example.json: instance_id removed, since nothing uses it now.

  Local smoke test (fake RabbitMQ and consumer, stand-in client jar, no SSH or
  CloudWatch)
  - The whole sequence ran: starting counts, queue sampling, client, drain wait,
    collection, summary, and summary.csv.
  - When the consumer processed every swipe and the queue emptied, the
    swipe-loss check said PASS. On the earlier run, where about 30,000 swipes
    appeared unprocessed, it said FAIL.
  - Not covered: host_monitor.py hasn't run anywhere yet, because it needs Linux
    and Docker wasn't running.

  Real smoke test on AWS: I don't have your SSH key or a filled-in
  test_config.json, so I can't run this one. After the setup in
  monitoring/README.md:
  python3 run_test.py --config test_config.json --label smoke-1srv \
      --base-url http://<alb-dns>/ski-servlet --threads 64 --swipes 82000
  --ingest server1
  That's 50K swipes plus the 32K warmup, and it will exercise SSH, the
  access-log counts and CloudWatch for the first time.

  The Part 4 targets: meets_queue_target covers queue depth < 1000, and
  loss_check covers no swipe loss. Consumer lag < 100 ms is not measured, only
  estimated, as covered last time.

  Leaderboard updates: what is and isn't shown

  Shown: the updates are real. LeaderboardEndpoint no longer has a synthetic
  generator; it only forwards boards the consumer broadcasts after applying
  swipes. Any update a subscriber receives is therefore caused by a swipe. The
  summary counts updates per run and flags any resort whose subscriber received
  none. (server-v2/README.md still says "pushes a synthetic top-10 leaderboard";
  that line is out of date.)

  Not shown:
  - Board contents aren't kept. LeaderboardSubscriber.recordMessage keeps only
    the serverTimestamp of each message and discards the board itself, so
    nothing compares skier verticals with the swipes sent.
  - Old boards are replayed. When a subscriber connects, the server first sends
    the latest board left in its memory from earlier runs (onOpen). A non-zero
    update count therefore isn't proof on its own.

  A check that needs no code: use a day the load test doesn't touch. The
  generator uses today's day of the year, so dayID=1 stays empty. Then:
  1. Subscribe: npx wscat -c ws://<alb-dns>/ski-servlet/leaderboard/1
  2. Send one swipe for a new skier on lift 6 (397 ft):
     curl -X POST 
     http://<alb-dns>/ski-servlet/skiers/1/seasons/2026/days/1/skiers/999999 -H 
     'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"liftID":6,"time":100}'
     The pushed board for dayID 1 should show skier 999999 with vertical 397.
  3. Send a second swipe on lift 3 (344 ft). The board should update to 741 with
     a higher version.

  A screenshot of that is direct evidence that the updates reflect the swipes.
  Run it once, alongside the load runs.

  Caveats that could understate or hide results

  1. Consumer lag isn't measured. Only the estimate est_drain_*_ms is reported,
     so the < 100 ms target can't be claimed.
  2. The rate averages run low. The averaging window starts when the client JVM
     launches. That includes up to 10 s of subscribers connecting and the
     32-thread warmup, both of which lower producer_rate_avg. consumer_rate_avg
     also includes the drain tail. For the report, compare producer_rate_avg
     with client_throughput, and quote the steady plateau from queue.csv.
  3. System metrics haven't been proven yet:
     - host_monitor.py has never run on Linux.
     - If SSH fails, the run continues with only a logged warning, so check that
       summary.json contains each <host>_cpu_* field after the first run.
     - The client machine itself isn't monitored. In the 1M stress test it could
       be the bottleneck without anything showing it.
  4. Per-server request counts depend on Tomcat's default access log being on.
     Check that distribution.csv isn't empty after the smoke test.

  Verdict

  - Client and queue metrics: meet the minimum.
  - System metrics: meet it on paper; the first real run will confirm it.
  - Leaderboard updates: the scripts prove real updates arrive, but not that
    they match the swipes. The manual check above closes that without code. The
    alternative is a small client change to keep the last board per subscriber,
    which I haven't made since you asked for no code.

  Problems found in this review

  1. The RabbitMQ login will probably fail as configured. The example config
     uses guest, and RabbitMQ only allows guest from the RabbitMQ machine
     itself. Each sample would then fail with a confusing "rabbit sample failed"
     error, and queue.csv would be empty. Use the skitrak user from the server
     README, and give it the monitoring tag so it can read the management API.
  2. The drain estimate misses its worst case. If the consumer stalls (ack rate
     0 while messages are queued), that sample's est_drain_ms is left blank.
     Blank samples are skipped in the averages, so the estimate looks better
     than reality exactly when the consumer is stuck.
  3. Per-server counts can come back as 0 without any error. This happens in two
     cases:
     - Tomcat isn't installed under ~/apache-tomcat-*.
     - The access log isn't enabled.

     Tomcat also writes that log in batches, so the last few seconds can be
     missing from the count. The drain wait usually covers that.
  4. cpu_pct counts steal and iowait as busy time. On a t3 instance that has run
     out of CPU credits, CPU will look near 100% even when the application is
     idle. Read it together with steal_pct.
  5. Missing host metrics only produce a warning. If SSH fails, the run
     continues with only a log line, and summary.json simply has no host fields.
     Check for them after the first run.
  6. The client machine itself isn't monitored. In the 1M stress test it could
     be the bottleneck without anything showing it.

  Accuracy limits

  - Rate averages: producer_rate_avg and consumer_rate_avg are averaged from the
    moment the client JVM starts. That includes up to 10 s of subscribers
    connecting and the 32-thread warmup, so both read low. In the report, set
    them beside client_throughput and the steady level in queue.csv.
  - Swipe-loss check: if a client retry makes the server publish the same swipe
    twice, the extra copy can offset a real loss of the same size. The
    dead-letter and drain checks are the backstop.

  Bottom line: every per-run metric the assignment lists is collected. Consumer
  lag < 100 ms can't be claimed without the consumer change. The "updates
  reflect swipes" part needs either the manual check or a small client change
  that keeps the latest leaderboard. Fix the RabbitMQ user (problem 1) before
  the first real run, or the queue metrics will be empty. Do you want me to make
  any of these changes?

  Two of the requested sweeps can't be done as written:
  1. Consumer threads 20/40/80: ConsumerConfig caps the thread count at the

  2. Batch ACK size: fixed at 1. Changing it needs a code change.

  write-up that 10 is the ceiling (one thread per queue, which keeps per-resort
  ordering). Batch ACK stays at 1 and is reported as fixed. Going beyond either
  limit needs consumer code changes, which is your call. I haven't made any.

  Tuning sequence

  Common to every run:
  - Load goes through the ALB with all 4 ingest servers. That gives the most
    producer pressure, so consumer limits actually show up.
  - 200K swipes plus the 32K warmup (--swipes 232000).
  - Run each configuration once; run it twice if two configurations come out
    within about 5%.
  - Before each run: queues empty, and CPU credits not exhausted.
  - Each run uses run_test.py, with the settings recorded in --notes.

  Step 0 – Prerequisites (no tuning yet)

  - Read the live settings:
    - cat /etc/skitrak/consumer.env on the consumer
    - setenv.sh on each server
    - curl :8081/health for the live consumer thread count
  - Turn off autoscaling (CONSUMER_AUTOSCALE=false). Otherwise the thread count
    changes during a run, and the consumer-thread sweep means nothing.
  - Use a non-guest RabbitMQ user with the monitoring tag in test_config.json.
    Otherwise queue.csv stays empty.
  - Run one smoke test to confirm queue.csv, hosts/*.csv and distribution.csv
    all fill in.

  Step 1 – Client ingest threads

  ┌───────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          Changes          │                    Fixed                    │
  ├───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ client threads 64 → 128 → │ consumer threads 10, prefetch 100, ACK      │
  │  256 → 512                │ batch 1, channel pool 64                    │
  └───────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Goal: find where adding threads stops helping, meaning throughput levels off
  while p99 latency, failures or retries jump. Expect a limit around 512
  threads: that's about 128 concurrent requests per server against 64 channels,
  so requests may wait for a channel. The winner (call it T*) is fixed for the
  following steps.

  Step 2 – Consumer threads

  ┌──────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │             Changes              │                Fixed                 │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ consumer threads 1 → 2 → 5 → 10  │ client threads T*, prefetch 100, ACK │
  │ (autoscaling off)                │  batch 1, channel pool 64            │
  └──────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘

  Goal: find the smallest thread count where the queue stays below 1000 and
  shows a plateau rather than a sawtooth, with no loss. Low thread counts should
  show the "bad" sawtooth and high counts the "good" plateau, which gives you
  both queue profiles for the report. The winner (C*) is fixed afterwards.

  Step 3 – Prefetch (only if Step 2 misses the queue target at C*)

  ┌──────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │           Changes            │                Fixed                 │
  ├──────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ prefetch 10 → 50 → 100 → 250 │ T*, C*, ACK batch 1, channel pool 64 │
  └──────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘

  Step 4 – Server channel pool (only if Step 1 shows failures or retries while 
  server CPU is low)

  ┌────────────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          Changes           │               Fixed                │
  ├────────────────────────────┼────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ channel pool 32 → 64 → 128 │ T*, C*, best prefetch, ACK batch 1 │
  └────────────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┘

  That's 8 runs, plus up to 7 more if Steps 3 or 4 are needed. The final
  configuration then feeds the 500K/1M performance tests.

⏺ Before the proposal, one problem that isn't about consumer threads. The
  consumer names its queues skitrak.resort.N (Topology.java:36), but
  monitoring/queue_monitor.py watches queues starting with resort., following
  deployment/config.md. Either it's watching nothing (depth always 0), or there
  are also manually created resort.N queues bound to the same routing keys. In
  that second case, every swipe is copied into a queue nobody reads, and after
  60 s those copies expire into the dead-letter queue, so every run's loss check
  fails. Please check the queue list in the RabbitMQ UI. Either way, the
  monitor's prefix has to change. That's change 6 below.

  Why the consumer stops at 10 threads

  Two things block it:
  1. The queues are declared with x-single-active-consumer: true
     (Topology.java). RabbitMQ delivers each queue's messages to only one
     consumer, so with 10 queues at most 10 threads do work, however many are
     started.
  2. ConsumerConfig caps maxWorkers at the number of resort queues (10), and
     ConsumerPool.assign maps each resort to one worker. Extra workers would get
     no queue at all.

  Proposed changes: several workers per queue

  With 20/40/80 threads, each queue gets 2, 4 or 8 competing consumers. Each
  worker keeps its own channel and thread, which is the existing design.

  #: 1
  File: consumer/.../Topology.java
  Change: Remove args.put("x-single-active-consumer", true)
  Why it's needed: Otherwise only one consumer per queue receives messages
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 2
  File: consumer/.../ConsumerConfig.java
  Change: Stop capping maxWorkers at the resort count. The default stays 10, so
  behavior is unchanged unless CONSUMER_THREADS/CONSUMER_MAX_THREADS are set
  Why it's needed: Allows 20, 40 and 80
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 3
  File: consumer/.../ConsumerPool.java
  Change: Make assign work in both directions: worker k % W subscribes to resort

  k % R for k = 0 … max(W, R) − 1. W=10 gives 1 queue each (same as now); W=80
  gives 8 workers per queue; W<10 gives a worker several queues (same as now).
  Update the resize log line to match.
  Why it's needed: Extra workers must get a queue
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 4
  File: consumer/.../TopN.java
  Change: In update, ignore a new total that isn't higher than the skier's
  current one
  Why it's needed: With two threads on one resort, a skier's swipes can be
  applied out of order: A computes total 100, B computes 200, B updates the
  board, then A writes 100 over it. Totals only grow, so the guard is exact.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 5
  File: consumer/.../LeaderboardAggregator.java
  Change: Mark the event as seen first with an atomic putIfAbsent, and remove
  the
  mark if applying the swipe throws
  Why it's needed: With competing consumers, a redelivered copy could be applied

  by two threads at once and be double-counted
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 6
  File: monitoring/queue_monitor.py
  Change: Change RESORT_PREFIX from "resort." to "skitrak.resort."
  Why it's needed: Otherwise queue depth, rates, the drain check and the loss
  check all read the wrong queues

  One step on the broker (no code): RabbitMQ rejects redeclaring an existing
  queue with different arguments (error 406 PRECONDITION_FAILED). So when no
  load is running:
  1. Stop the consumer.
  2. Delete the 10 skitrak.resort.N queues (and any stray resort.N queues) in
     the management UI.
  3. Deploy the new consumer jar. On startup it recreates and rebinds the queues
     without single-active-consumer.

  Left unchanged: batch ACK stays at 1, prefetch at 100, and the server's
  channel pool at 64. The server and client code don't change; client threads
  are already a command-line argument.

  Side effects to know about:
  - Order within a resort is no longer guaranteed. Totals stay correct, because
    vertical is a sum and order doesn't affect it. Change 4 keeps the board from
    going backwards.
  - Out-of-order leaderboard broadcasts are a bit more likely. Two threads on
    one resort can send their snapshots in either order, so a subscriber may
    briefly see an older board. Earlier you decided against a version check, on
    the grounds that there's a single consumer process. That's still true, but
    within that process snapshot order is now not guaranteed. I'm leaving it as
    you decided unless you say otherwise.
  - The extra threads may not help. The consumer is a 2-vCPU t3.micro, so beyond
    some point more threads may give no gain or even lower throughput. That
    would be a valid finding to report.
  - Queue depth includes in-flight messages. At 80 threads × prefetch 100, up to
    8,000 messages can be handed out but not yet acked, and those count toward
    the depth. Read high-thread results with that in mind.

  Tuning sequence after the change

  Fixed for every run:
  - prefetch 100, batch ACK 1, channel pool 64
  - 4 ingest servers behind the ALB
  - CONSUMER_AUTOSCALE=false
  - --swipes 232000 (200K plus warmup)

  Step: 1 – Client threads
  Changes: 64, 128, 256, 512
  Also fixed: consumer threads 10
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Step: 2 – Consumer threads
  Changes: 10, 20, 40, 80 (set CONSUMER_THREADS=CONSUMER_MAX_THREADS=N, restart,

  confirm the thread count on /health)
  Also fixed: client threads = the best value from Step 1

  Each run goes through run_test.py with the settings in --notes. Read these
  fields:
  - queue depth: queue_depth_peak / meets_queue_target / queue_depth_cycles
  - swipe loss: loss_check
  - consumer lag: est_drain_*_ms, which is an estimate only, so the < 100 ms
    target won't be claimed as verified

  Should I go ahead with changes 1–6? I'd also build the consumer jar to confirm
  it compiles. The broker step and the deployment would be yours to run.

⏺ The consumer can now run 10, 20, 40 or 80 threads, all doing real work. The
  JAR is built at consumer/target/ski-consumer-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar, and nothing has
  been deployed. Before the new JAR will start on EC2, the existing queues have
  to be deleted (details at the end).

  Files changed

  consumer/ and monitoring/ aren't tracked by git yet, so there's no git diff to
  show; this list is complete.

  File: consumer/src/main/java/consumer/Topology.java
  Change: Removed x-single-active-consumer, so several workers can read the same

  queue.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: consumer/src/main/java/consumer/ConsumerConfig.java
  Change: Removed the 10-thread cap. The default is still 10.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: consumer/src/main/java/consumer/ConsumerPool.java
  Change: assign now gives each worker its list of queues, using round-robin
  across all workers and queues. Removed the byWorker helper, which only the
  old assign used.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: consumer/src/main/java/consumer/TopN.java
  Change: update ignores a total that isn't higher than the skier's current one
  (before, it only ignored an equal total).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: consumer/src/main/java/consumer/LeaderboardAggregator.java
  Change: Checking and marking a swipe as seen is now one atomic step
  (putIfAbsent), and the mark is removed if applying the swipe fails.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: monitoring/queue_monitor.py
  Change: Queue name prefix resort. → skitrak.resort. to match the consumer.
  Without this, every queue metric read the wrong queues.

  Batch ACK (1), prefetch (100) and the channel pool (64) are unchanged, and no
  client or server code was touched.

  Verification

  The consumer has no test suite, so I checked the built classes with a
  throwaway program in the scratchpad, outside the repo.

  - Worker assignment: at 10, 20, 40 and 80 threads, every worker gets exactly
    one queue, all 10 queues are covered, and each queue gets 1, 2, 4 or 8
    consumers. No worker subscribes to the same queue twice.
  - Leaderboard guard: a skier's lower total never replaces a higher one, both
    one at a time and across 200 rounds of 8 threads applying out-of-order
    totals. update is synchronized, so the guard is thread-safe.
  - Duplicates: 16 threads delivered the same 2,000 swipes at once. Each was
    applied exactly once, 30,000 were reported as duplicates, and the skier's
    total came out exact.
  - Failed apply: when applying a swipe throws, the swipe is no longer marked as
    seen, so the worker's retry is processed rather than treated as a
    duplicate.

  Limitations

  - Queues must be recreated before deploying. RabbitMQ refuses to redeclare an
    existing queue with different settings (error 406). Stop the consumer,
    delete the 10 skitrak.resort.N queues, then start the new JAR, which
    recreates them. I didn't touch RabbitMQ. Please also check whether stray
    resort.N queues exist (from deployment/config.md); if they do, they collect
    a copy of every swipe that nobody reads.
  - Each test needs three env settings: CONSUMER_THREADS=N,
    CONSUMER_MAX_THREADS=N and CONSUMER_AUTOSCALE=false. Without the last one,
    the autoscaler changes the thread count mid-run. Then check the threads
    value on /health.
  - Order within a resort is no longer guaranteed. Totals stay exact, because
    they're sums. Leaderboard snapshots from different threads can still arrive
    out of order, so a subscriber may briefly see an older board. That's
    unchanged, per your earlier decision against a version check.
  - Two older edge cases become slightly more likely with several threads per
    resort:
    - A snapshot's version and its entries are read separately, so they can come
      from different moments.
    - If applying a swipe throws after the total was added, the retry adds it
      again. Nothing in that path throws RuntimeException in practice.
  - High thread counts inflate queue depth. At 80 threads × prefetch 100, up to
    8,000 messages can be in flight unacked, and those count toward queue depth.
    The consumer is also a 2-vCPU t3.micro, so 40 or 80 threads may be no
    faster than 20.

❯ give me the deployment steps for the new consumer jar                         
⏺ I'll check whether the ingest servers also declare the resort queues. If they

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The ingest servers only declare the exchanges, never the resort queues, so
  they don't need a restart. Only the consumer and the queues change. Do this
  while no load test is running: while the queues are deleted, any swipe
  published to the exchange is routed nowhere and lost.

  Replace the placeholders <key>.pem, <CONSUMER_IP> and <RMQ_USER>:<RMQ_PASS>.
  The RabbitMQ private IP is 172.31.44.149.

  1. Copy the JAR (from your laptop, at the repo root)

  scp -i <key>.pem consumer/target/ski-consumer-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar
  ec2-user@<CONSUMER_IP>:~

  2. Stop the consumer (on the consumer instance)

  ssh -i <key>.pem ec2-user@<CONSUMER_IP>
  sudo systemctl stop skitrak-consumer

  3. Delete the old resort queues

  The new JAR declares the queues without single-active-consumer. If the old
  queues still exist, RabbitMQ refuses the declaration with PRECONDITION_FAILED
  (406), the consumer exits, and systemd keeps restarting it.

  First list the queues, to see which exist and whether they're empty:
  curl -s -u <RMQ_USER>:<RMQ_PASS> http://172.31.44.149:15672/api/queues/%2F \
    | python3 -c 'import json,sys; [print(q["name"], q.get("messages",0)) for q 
  in json.load(sys.stdin)]'

  Then delete them. if-empty=true makes RabbitMQ refuse to delete a queue that
  still has messages:
  for i in $(seq 1 10); do
    curl -s -o /dev/null -w "skitrak.resort.$i -> %{http_code}\n" -u
  <RMQ_USER>:<RMQ_PASS> -X DELETE \

  "http://172.31.44.149:15672/api/queues/%2F/skitrak.resort.$i?if-empty=true"
  done
  - 204 means deleted.
  - 400 means the queue still has messages. Either wait or purge it, then rerun
    the loop.
  - If the listing also shows plain resort.1…resort.10 queues, delete them the
    same way. Nothing reads them, but they take a copy of every swipe.

  If port 15672 isn't reachable from the consumer instance, do the same in the
  management UI: Queues → select a queue → Delete.

  4. Install the JAR (back up the old one first)

  sudo cp /opt/skitrak/ski-consumer-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar
  /opt/skitrak/ski-consumer-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar.bak
  sudo install -m 644 ~/ski-consumer-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar
  /opt/skitrak/ski-consumer-1.0-SNAPSHOT.jar

  5. Set the tuning variables

  Edit the env file with sudo vi /etc/skitrak/consumer.env. Keep the existing
  RABBITMQ_* lines and add or update these:
  CONSUMER_THREADS=10
  CONSUMER_MAX_THREADS=10
  CONSUMER_AUTOSCALE=false
  CONSUMER_PREFETCH=100

  6. Start and check the logs

  sudo systemctl start skitrak-consumer
  journalctl -u skitrak-consumer -n 30 --no-pager
  The log should show consumer threads 0 -> 10, resorts per thread {0=[1], 
  1=[2], …} and no PRECONDITION_FAILED.

  7. Verify

  - Consumer health: curl -s localhost:8081/health should show "status":"UP" and
    "threads":10.
  - Queues and bindings in the management UI:
    - skitrak.resort.1…10 exist, and their Features column no longer shows SAC.
    - Each queue shows 1 consumer.
    - The Bindings tab of skitrak.exchange shows resort.N → skitrak.resort.N.
  - One test swipe through the ALB, after which swipesProcessed on /health
    should go up by 1:
  curl -X POST
  http://<alb-dns>/ski-servlet/skiers/1/seasons/2026/days/1/skiers/999999 \
       -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"liftID":6,"time":100}'

  Changing the thread count for the sweep (20, 40, 80)

  Set CONSUMER_THREADS and CONSUMER_MAX_THREADS to the same N, then:
  sudo systemctl restart skitrak-consumer
  curl -s localhost:8081/health          # "threads": N
  In the UI, each queue should show N/10 consumers (2, 4 or 8). The queues don't
  need deleting again; that was a one-time step.

  Rollback

  1. sudo systemctl stop skitrak-consumer
  2. Restore the .bak JAR.
  3. Delete the 10 queues again, because the old JAR declares them with
     single-active-consumer.
  4. Start the service.

⏺ I'll re-read the current consumer code, and look for the assignment's own

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The current consumer conflicts with Assignment 2, and the conflict comes from
  my last change. Removing single-active-consumer to make 20/40/80 threads do
  work broke the Part 2 requirement to preserve per-resort ordering ("Assign
  each resort queue to one sequential processing worker at a time; allow
  different resort queues to run concurrently"). I didn't modify anything in
  this review.

  Current behavior

  1. Topology.java: single-active-consumer is off. I removed
  x-single-active-consumer in the last change, so RabbitMQ no longer limits a
  resort queue to one active consumer. Your broker's queues may differ from the
  code: if you haven't run the deployment steps yet, the existing
  skitrak.resort.N queues still have it on.

  2. ConsumerPool.java: yes, several workers consume the same queue 
  concurrently. assign gives worker k % W the queue for resort k % 10, so at
  20/40/80 threads each queue has 2/4/8 consumers. Without
  single-active-consumer, RabbitMQ splits each queue's messages among them and
  they process in parallel. At 10 threads or fewer, each queue has exactly one
  subscriber from this process, but nothing at the broker enforces that.

  3. ConsumerConfig.java: thread counts need two settings. You set the count
  with CONSUMER_THREADS=N and CONSUMER_MAX_THREADS=N.
  - maxWorkers defaults to 10. If only CONSUMER_THREADS=40 is set, it's quietly
    reduced to 10.
  - CONSUMER_AUTOSCALE defaults to true, so for a pinned count it must be set to
    false.

  4. Dynamic scaling: it works, but slowly and with pauses.
  - Every 5 s the autoscaler adds one thread if the backlog is above 2000 ×
    threads, and removes one if it's below 100, within [min, max].
  - It moves one step per interval, so going from 10 to 80 takes about 70 steps,
    roughly 6 minutes.
  - Every resize stops all workers one after another (each waits up to 10 s to
    finish its current message) and recreates every channel. Consumption pauses
    on each step, and unacked messages are requeued; duplicate detection absorbs
    the redeliveries.
  - It reacts to load, but in a load test the pauses can make the backlog worse
    rather than better.

  5. Per-resort ordering: not guaranteed.
  - Above 10 threads: competing consumers on one queue apply its messages in
    parallel, so queue order is lost.
  - At 10 or fewer: order holds in steady state, but only because of how this
    one process assigns queues. A second consumer process, or an overlapping
    restart, would split a queue.
  - Totals stay correct either way. Vertical is a sum, and the TopN guard stops
    the board from going backwards. But Part 2 asks for queue order, not just
    correct totals.

  The conflict

  There are 10 resort queues, and Part 2 requires each one to be processed by a
  single sequential worker. Strict per-resort ordering therefore caps real
  parallelism at 10. Part 4 requires testing 10/20/40/80 consumer threads. Both
  requirements can be met only if threads 11–80 exist and are tested, but don't
  process a resort queue at the same time as its owner.

  Smallest change that satisfies both

  Turn single-active-consumer back on, and keep everything else from the last 
  change.

  ┌─────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │          File           │                    Change                     │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Topology.java           │ Put args.put("x-single-active-consumer",      │
  │                         │ true) back (undoes change 1).                 │
  ├─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ConsumerConfig.java /   │ Comments only: say that workers beyond the    │
  │ ConsumerPool.java       │ queue count register as standbys.             │
  └─────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  Kept from the last change:
  - Generalized assign: 20/40/80 threads still start and subscribe across all 10
    queues.
  - No 10-thread cap: the counts can still be configured.
  - TopN guard and atomic duplicate check: both are harmless and defensive.

  Effect:
  - Ordering is enforced by RabbitMQ. Each queue delivers to exactly one active
    consumer, whatever the thread count and however many consumer processes run.
    That satisfies Part 2 word for word.
  - 20/40/80 are real, testable configurations. On each queue, the first
    subscriber is active and the rest wait as standbys. If the active consumer's
    channel dies, RabbitMQ promotes a standby, so the extra threads add
    failover rather than throughput.
  - Part 4 results will show where the limit is. Throughput should level off at
    10 threads, which is a valid finding: strict per-resort ordering limits
    parallelism to the number of queues. It also removes an earlier caveat:
    standbys hold no prefetched messages, so queue depth is no longer inflated
    at 80 threads.
  - Dynamic scaling keeps working unchanged. Each resize stops all old consumers
    before any new ones subscribe, so a resize can't reorder messages either.
    The slow one-step rate and the pause on each resize are worth a sentence in
    the report, but they don't conflict with the requirements.
  - Deployment gets simpler if you haven't redeployed yet. The existing queues
    already have single-active-consumer, so the queue-deletion step from my
    deployment instructions is no longer needed. If you already recreated them
    without it, delete and recreate them once more.

  The alternative, if you want 20/40/80 to add throughput: split each resort
  into several queues by skierID (skitrak.resort.N.k). That needs a server
  routing change, and it only keeps ordering per skier, not per resort, so it
  would break the Part 2 requirement as written. I don't recommend it.

  Kept from the previous change:
  - The thread count can go above 10. CONSUMER_THREADS=N and
    CONSUMER_MAX_THREADS=N accept 10, 20, 40 or 80.

  duplicates are applied only once.

  Limits:
  - The standby behavior is untested. It's RabbitMQ's job and needs a real
    broker; Docker wasn't running here. After deploying, the management UI page
    for skitrak.resort.1 should show single-active-consumer in its features and
    N/10 consumers, with only one marked active.
  - Threads beyond 10 add failover, not throughput. Expect results to level off
    at 10 threads; that comes from strict per-resort ordering with 10 queues.
  - Dynamic scaling is as before. It moves one step every 5 s. Above 10 threads
    it only adds standbys, and each resize briefly pauses consumption.

  Deployment:
  - If you haven't recreated the queues yet, skip step 3 of my deployment steps.
    The existing queues already have this setting and match the new JAR.
  - If you already recreated them without it, delete them once more (consumer
    stopped, queues empty) before starting this JAR, or it will fail with

  Round 1: 10

  Round 1: 1

  Round 1: 64
  Round 2: 64

  Parameter: Queue topology
  How it's set: consumer Topology.java: single-active-consumer, TTL, max-length,

  dead-lettering
  Round 1: unchanged
  Round 2: unchanged

  Pinning the thread count during tuning: the default autoscaler would distort
  the runs. With min=1 and an idle queue, it removes a thread every 5 s, so "10
  threads" would already be fewer by the time the load arrives. For each tuning
  run I propose CONSUMER_THREADS = CONSUMER_MIN_THREADS = CONSUMER_MAX_THREADS =
  N. The autoscaler keeps running but has no room to move, so its code path
  stays active and the count stays fixed.

  The final configuration will have a real min/max range with autoscaling on,
  followed by one validation run (step 5 below).

  Changes needed (all small, monitoring scripts only)

  File: monitoring/run_test.py
  Change: Record the consumer's live threads value from /health before and after

  each run, and warn if they differ.
  Why: Today the consumer configuration exists only as free text in --notes.
  This
  proves each run actually used its intended thread count.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: monitoring/summarize.py
  Change: (a) Add a consumer_threads column. (b) Average producer and consumer
  rates only over samples where swipes were flowing. (c) Add --compare, which
  writes results/comparison.csv and prints the best configuration per round
  using the rule below.
  Why: (a) needed to compare runs; (b) the current averages include the
  subscriber-connect phase and idle samples, so they read low; (c) gives the
  requested comparison summary.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: monitoring/README.md
  Change: A short tuning section with the procedure and label scheme.
  Why: Documentation.

  Unchanged: client, server, consumer code, queue topology, queue_monitor.py,
  host_monitor.py.

  Measured vs. estimated:
  - Measured: runtime, throughput, failures, retries and latency (client);
    producer and consumer rates and queue depth (RabbitMQ API); CPU and memory
    (/proc).
  - Estimated: est_drain_*_ms is the only estimate. The comparison will label it
    "estimated drain time, not consumer lag".
  - Not measured: consumer lag. The report will say so, since the consumer
    doesn't record enqueue-to-processing time.

  Best-configuration rule (fixed before any results)

  1. A run qualifies only if:
     - loss_check is PASS,
     - client_failed is 0,
     - the consumer thread count matched the intended value at start and end.
  2. Among qualifying runs, the highest client_throughput wins.
  3. If two runs are within 5% of each other, the lower latency_p99_ms wins; if
     still tied, the lower queue_depth_peak.
  4. Round 2 only: if several consumer counts are within 5%, the smallest thread
     count wins. Throughput is expected to level off at 10, because
     single-active-consumer allows one active consumer per queue.

  Execution plan

  Same conditions for every run:
  - 4 ingest servers behind the ALB.
  - --swipes 232000: 200K main-phase swipes plus the 32K warmup.
  - prefetch 100, batch ACK 1, channel pool 64.
  - Before each run:
    - restart the consumer, so its in-memory state and counters start fresh,
    - confirm the queues are empty,
    - check that no host shows high CPU steal (a t3 instance that has used up
      its CPU credits shows up as steal).

  Labels: r1-c064-k10 … r1-c512-k10 and r2-c<best>-k10 … r2-c<best>-k80 (c =
  client threads, k = consumer threads), each in its own results/<label>/.

  0. Prerequisites, done by you:
     - deploy the current consumer JAR,
     - create test_config.json with the SSH key and a non-guest RabbitMQ user,
     - open ports 15672 and 8081 to the machine that runs the tests.
  1. Smoke test, after your approval: one 50K run (--swipes 82000, 64 client
     threads, 10 consumer threads). It checks that the queue, host and
     request-count files all fill in. It doesn't count toward tuning.
  2. Round 1: 4 runs at 64, 128, 256 and 512 client threads, consumer pinned
     at 10.
  3. Round 2: 4 runs at 10, 20, 40 and 80 consumer threads with the best client
     thread count. Restart the consumer and check threads on /health before each
     run.
  4. Compare: summarize.py --compare results produces the per-round tables and
     the winners.
  5. Final configuration:
     - CONSUMER_AUTOSCALE=true,
     - CONSUMER_THREADS and CONSUMER_MAX_THREADS set to the Round 2 winner,
     - CONSUMER_MIN_THREADS to be decided by you (next question).

     Then one 232K validation run with autoscaling on, to show queue depth and
     loss still pass.

  That's 10 AWS runs, plus the optional repeat of any winner within 5%. Each
  runs only after your go-ahead.

  Two decisions I need:
  1. Workload per tuning run: OK with 200K main-phase swipes (--swipes 232000),
     or do you want 500K?
  2. Minimum consumer threads for the final configuration:
     - (a) MIN=10: keeps one active consumer per queue at all times. Scaling
       then only adds standbys, so it's safe but has little visible effect.
     - (b) A lower minimum, e.g. 5: scaling visibly changes how many consumers
       are active. Ramping up at the start of a load test takes about 5 s per
       thread, so queue depth may spike.

     I recommend (a) for meeting the assignment targets, and describing the
     scaling behavior in the report.

  Should I make the three script changes once you've answered?

  Three decisions before I run

  1. Where the client runs. From this Mac, every request crosses the internet to
     us-west-2, so network latency, not the system, could be what limits
     throughput. That's especially true at 256 and 512 threads. Running the
     client on an EC2 instance in us-west-2 gives truer numbers, but launching
     one is an infrastructure change.
     - (a) I launch a temporary t3.small client instance and terminate it
       afterwards. Recommended.
     - (b) Run from this Mac and note the limitation in the report.
  2. Batch ACK size. Last time you said to keep it at 1; now it's on the tuning
     list. The ACK is hard-coded, so tuning it needs a consumer change:
     - a CONSUMER_ACK_BATCH setting,
     - basicAck(tag, multiple=true) every N messages,
     - the pending batch is sent whenever the worker's local queue is empty, so
       the batch can never wait forever for more deliveries once prefetch is
       full.

     Ordering is unaffected: single-active-consumer stays on, and redelivered
     messages are caught by the existing duplicate check. The default stays 1.
     OK to make and deploy that change?
  3. t3 CPU credits. Roughly 20 back-to-back runs on t3.micro instances can use
     up their CPU credits, after which they're throttled. That would bias the
     later runs. Options: switch the instances to "unlimited" mode (an
     infrastructure change, small cost), or pause between runs and record CPU
     steal, so throttled runs can be identified and rerun.

  Notes:
  - Batch ACK values follow the chosen prefetch. A batch bigger than the
    prefetch only ever gets sent early, when the worker runs out of messages. If
    prefetch 10 wins Round 3, Round 4 uses 1, 5 and 10 instead.
  - Reused baselines could drift. If a round's winner beats its reused baseline
    by less than 5%, I rerun the baseline once so both are measured under the
    same conditions. That's at most one extra run per round, done only when
    needed.
  - Picking each winner: the run must have no loss and no failed swipes, and its
    consumer thread count must be confirmed. Then highest throughput wins.
    Among runs within 5% of each other, the lower p99 latency wins; in Round 2,
    the fewest consumer threads win.
  - Expected results: Round 2 will likely show no gain beyond 10 threads, since
    single-active-consumer allows one active consumer per queue. I'll report
    that as the finding it is.

  - But every EC2 request is refused by the policy voc-cancel-cred. AWS Academy

    longer active, to cancel them.

  This usually means one of three things:
  1. The lab session has ended. The Learner Lab timer ran out, or the session
     was stopped.
  2. The keys came from an earlier session. Pressing "Start Lab" again issues
     new keys and cancels the old ones, so a block copied before the restart
     stops working.
  3. The lab budget is used up. AWS Academy then cancels all credentials.

  What to do:
  1. Open the Learner Lab page and check that the AWS indicator next to "AWS" is
     green. If not, press Start Lab and wait for it to turn green.
  2. Then open AWS Details → AWS CLI → Show, copy the block, and replace
     ~/.aws/credentials with it.
  3. If it's green and copying fresh keys still gives this error, check the
     budget shown on that page. If it's used up, only your instructor can
     restore access.

  Exact commands per N: the original file has none of these four keys, so this
  only adds lines and never touches the 7 existing ones:
  sudo sed -i -E '/^CONSUMER_(THREADS|MIN_THREADS|MAX_THREADS|PREFETCH)=/d'
  /etc/skitrak/consumer.env
  printf 'CONSUMER_THREADS=%s\nCONSUMER_MIN_THREADS=%s\nCONSUMER_MAX_THREADS=%s\
  nCONSUMER_PREFETCH=100\n' N N N \
    | sudo tee -a /etc/skitrak/consumer.env > /dev/null
  sudo grep -E '^CONSUMER_' /etc/skitrak/consumer.env     # show only the 
  CONSUMER_ keys; the password isn't printed
  - The sed line removes only these four keys, so switching N doesn't pile up
    duplicate lines.
  - Before the first edit, I'll check (read-only) that the file ends with a
    newline. Otherwise the first added key would be glued onto the password
    line.

  At 20/40/80, single-active-consumer still allows one active consumer per
  queue: 10 active consumers, and the rest (10/30/70) are standbys.

  2. Autoscaling during the runs

  It isn't disabled; it's pinned. CONSUMER_AUTOSCALE keeps its default of true.
  Setting MIN = MAX = N means the autoscaler can't move the count:
  - ConsumerConfig starts the pool at CONSUMER_THREADS, limited to [MIN, MAX],
    so at N.
  - Every 5 s the autoscaler asks for one more or one fewer thread.
    ConsumerPool.resize limits the target to [MIN, MAX] = [N, N] and returns
    without doing anything when the target equals the current size. The count
    stays at N, and no resize (with its pause and redelivery) ever happens.

  It's checked on every run, not assumed: run_test.py --consumer-threads N
  compares N with the live threads value on /health at the start and end of the
  run. A mismatch prints a warning and excludes the run from the comparison.

  If you'd rather switch it off explicitly, I can add CONSUMER_AUTOSCALE=false
  to the per-run keys. The result is the same thread count, and option (b)
  restores the default afterwards either way. Tell me if you prefer that.

  3. Prefetch and batch ACK

  - Prefetch: CONSUMER_PREFETCH (ConsumerConfig: intValue(source, 
    "CONSUMER_PREFETCH", 100)). It's applied per worker channel with
    basicQos(100). 100 is also the current default, so writing it out changes
    nothing. Standbys hold no messages, because single-active-consumer delivers
    only to the active consumer.
  - Batch ACK: 1. There's no environment variable for it. ResortWorker acks each
    message after processing (basicAck(tag, false)), and that stays as is
    because the JAR isn't changing.

  4. Drain check before every run

  Before each restart and each run, all three must hold:
  - every skitrak.resort.* queue has messages = 0 (ready plus unacked), read via
    the RabbitMQ API;
  - the consumer's /health shows backlog = 0;
  - the dead-letter queue count is recorded, so the run's loss check uses only
    what changes during the run.

  If any queue isn't empty: I wait and recheck. I don't restart or start the
  run, and I never purge. If it stays non-empty, I stop and report to you.

  After each run: run_test.py keeps monitoring until the queues are empty and
  the processed count stops changing (up to 300 s) before saving results. The
  next run's checks above then apply again.

  Restore after all experiments: option (b)

  1. Confirm the queues are drained.
  2. sudo cp -p /etc/skitrak/consumer.env.orig-20261102 
     /etc/skitrak/consumer.env, then confirm it's identical to the backup with
     cmp.
  3. sudo systemctl restart skitrak-consumer.
  4. Confirm the defaults are back: the log shows the pool starting at 5
     threads, and the autoscaler then reducing it toward 1 while idle, exactly
     as before.

  The backup file stays on the instance unless you ask me to delete it.

  Waiting for your final approval before any change or restart.

  Chosen test values, from the code

  Prefetch: 10, 100 (baseline), 250.
  - Why it matters: with single-active-consumer, each queue's one active
    consumer handles messages one at a time. Prefetch only decides how many
    messages wait in its local buffer.
  - 10: tests whether a small buffer starves the worker while acks travel to
    RabbitMQ and back.
  - 250: tests whether a larger buffer helps.
  - Effect on queue depth: unacked messages count toward queue depth, so a
    larger prefetch can raise the measured depth without any real backlog.

  Batch ACK: 1 (baseline), 10, 50.
  - Must not exceed prefetch. If Round 3 picks prefetch 10, Round 4 uses 1, 5
    and 10 instead.
  - The proposed implementation (it needs the code change):
    - each worker acks with basicAck(lastTag, multiple=true) after every B
      processed messages;
    - it always sends the pending batch when its local queue is empty, before a
      reject, and when stopping, so a batch never waits forever and nothing
      processed stays unacked;
    - a message is only acked after it's been processed.
  - Why it's safe: each channel is used by a single thread, which processes
    deliveries in delivery-tag order. "All tags up to here" therefore covers
    only processed messages. A rejected message is already settled, so a later
    multi-ack doesn't touch it.
  - If a channel closes mid-batch, RabbitMQ redelivers the processed-but-unacked
    messages, and the duplicate check absorbs them. They show up as duplicates,
    not as loss or double counts.

  Channel pool: 32, 64 (baseline), 128.
  - Concurrency per server: with T client threads spread over 4 servers, each
    server handles about T/4 requests at once (16 to 128 for T = 64 to 512).
    Tomcat's default maxThreads is 200.
  - The two ends: a pool of 32 may make requests wait for a channel (up to 2 s)
    at high client concurrency. 128 covers even 512 client threads.
  - The broker can handle it: 4 servers × 128 channels = 512 channels, far below
    RabbitMQ's per-connection limit.

  Consumer threads: 10, 20, 40, 80 (required), plus 5, 10, 15, 20 as an optional
  extra round.
  - With single-active-consumer and 10 queues, at most 10 consumers are ever
    active.
  - 5 means 5 active consumers, each handling 2 queues.
  - 20/40/80 means 10 active consumers plus 10/30/70 standbys.
  - Expected result: no change above 10. That's a finding to report, not a
    failure.

  Experiment matrix (one factor at a time)

  Workload: 232,000 swipes total per tuning run (32K warmup + 200K main), sent
  from this Mac through the ALB to all 4 ingest servers.

  Bold values are reused from the previous round's winner rather than rerun. If

  When the new JAR goes in: I propose deploying the batch-ACK JAR once, before 
  Round 0. With CONSUMER_ACK_BATCH unset it behaves exactly like today (ACK per
  message), so every round runs on the same build, and the binary doesn't change
  halfway through.

  Picking each round's winner:
  - A run qualifies only if:
    - loss_check is PASS,
    - there are 0 failed swipes,
    - the dead-letter queue doesn't grow,
    - the live settings were confirmed.
  - Winner: the highest throughput. Among runs within 5% of it, the lower p99
    latency wins, then the lower peak queue depth.
  - Special cases:
    - in Round 2, the fewest consumer threads win among runs within 5%;
    - in Rounds 3–5, the baseline value wins a tie, to avoid changing a setting
      for no measurable gain.

  Safety checks for every run

  1. Before any config change, restart or run:
     - all 10 resort queues hold 0 messages and the consumer's backlog is 0;
     - the DLQ count is recorded;
     - if any of this fails, I stop. No purge, no retry.
  2. Config change: every edited file is backed up once first. I diff against
     the backup to confirm only the intended keys changed. Passwords never
     appear in any output.
  3. Live verification after a restart:
     - consumer threads: threads on /health;
     - prefetch: prefetch_count for each consumer in RabbitMQ's consumer list
       (read-only API);
     - ACK batch: the running process's environment (/proc/<pid>/environ) plus
       the startup log line;
     - pool size: each skitrak-server connection's channel count in RabbitMQ;
     - a mismatch stops everything.
  4. During and after the run: run_test.py waits for the queues to drain. The
     run fails if the DLQ grew or loss_check isn't PASS, and then I stop before
     the next run.
  5. Restarts happen only on drained queues. Tomcat restarts go one server at a
     time, and only while no test is running.

⏺ Here's the updated plan with all four points. I only read code; nothing was
  edited, built, deployed or run.

  1. Measuring consumer lag directly

  Ingest server (SwipeEventPublisher.publish): add one header to the message
  properties that are already built before basicPublish:
  .headers(Map.of("x-publish-ms", System.currentTimeMillis()))
  - Why a custom header: AMQP's built-in timestamp property only stores whole
    seconds, which is too coarse for a 100 ms target.
  - Retries: the properties are built once before the retry loop, so a publish
    retry keeps the first attempt's time. Lag then honestly includes publish
    retries.
  - Nothing else changes: the message body, routing and the existing
    SwipeEvent.timestamp field stay as they are.

  Consumer (ResortWorker + one new small class LagStats):
  - Read: handleDelivery reads x-publish-ms from props.getHeaders() and passes
    it with the delivery.
  - Record: when the worker starts processing that message, it records now − 
    publish time.
  - Why processing start, not arrival at the consumer: with prefetch, messages
    can wait in the worker's local buffer after RabbitMQ delivers them.
    Recording at arrival would hide that wait. Processing start is the stricter,
    more honest definition of "consumed". If you specifically want arrival
    time, it's a one-line move.
  - Storage: LagStats is a fixed histogram with 1 ms buckets from 0 to 60,000 ms
    (the queue TTL is 60 s, so nothing waits longer), plus an exact maximum. It
    costs one atomic increment per message.
  - New /health fields: lagSamples, lagMissingHeader (messages without the
    header), lagNegative (clock skew made the value negative; recorded as 0),
    lagP50Ms, lagP99Ms and lagMaxMs. The consumer is restarted before every run,
    so these are per-run values.

  This is instrumentation only: no change to processing, ordering, ACK timing or
  the queue setup.

  Clock sync check (read-only, before the runs):
  - What: on the consumer and all 4 servers, run chronyc tracking (system time
    offset, reference) and timedatectl show -p NTPSynchronized.
  - Why it matters: lag compares two machines' clocks, so the uncertainty is the
    server's offset plus the consumer's offset.
  - What we expect: Amazon Time Sync (169.254.169.123) normally keeps offsets
    under 1 ms.
  - If the offsets add up to more than 10 ms, lag results are marked unreliable
    instead of reported as PASS or FAIL.

  2. Queue depth as a time series

  - Collection: every run already records queue.csv. Sampling goes from every 2
    s to every 1 s. Depth is RabbitMQ's messages (ready plus unacked) summed
    over the 10 resort queues.
  - Summary: queue_depth_max and queue_depth_p95 over the load window.
  - Plot: summarize.py writes queue_depth.svg for each run: depth over time, a
    line at 1000, and the run's profile label in the title. It's generated with
    the standard library only.
  - Label: computed over the load window, relative to the run's own peak:
    - flat if the peak is under 50 messages;
    - sawtooth if depth rises above 50% of the peak and falls below 20% of it at
      least twice;
    - plateau otherwise.
  - Known limit: RabbitMQ refreshes queue statistics only every 5 s by default
    (collect_statistics_interval). Real resolution is about 5 s even with 1 s
    polling, so a short spike between refreshes can be missed. I'll check the
    broker's actual setting read-only (needs the RabbitMQ IP) and state the
    resolution in the report.

  3. Pass/fail on the three Part 4 targets, per run

  Target: Queue depth
  Rule: queue_depth_max < 1000
  Source: queue.csv (measured)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Target: Consumer lag
  Rule: lagP99Ms < 100
  Source: consumer /health (measured). Reported as NOT MEASURED if there are no
  samples, if less than 99% of processed messages carried the header, or if the
   clocks aren't reliably in sync
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Target: Zero loss
  Rule: loss_check PASS: client successes = processed, DLQ didn't grow, queues
  drained
  Source: run.json (measured)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Target: Overall
  Rule: PASS only if all three are PASS
  Source:

  est_drain_*_ms stays only as a secondary column labeled "estimated drain
  time". It's never used for the lag target.

  4. Consumer threads and connection pool

  - Consumer threads: one active consumer per queue stays
    (single-active-consumer on). At 20, 40 and 80 the extra threads are
    standbys, and the report states that result plainly, even if throughput
    doesn't change.
  - The pool being tuned: the ingest server's RabbitMQ publisher channel pool
    (ChannelPool). Each server opens one AMQP connection holding a fixed number
    of channels, RABBITMQ_CHANNEL_POOL_SIZE (live: 64 on all 4 servers). Each
    POST borrows a channel to publish. Not tuned:
    - the client's HTTP connection pool (Java HttpClient default, unbounded);
    - the consumer's connection (one connection, one channel per worker);
    - Tomcat's request threads (default 200).

  Client side:
  - Swipes: 50,000 OK, 0 failed, 0 retries.
  - Runtime and throughput: 33.4 s, 1,498 swipes/s.
  - Latency: mean 26.3 ms, p95 32.7 ms, p99 37.4 ms.
  - Rates: producer 1,441/s, consumer 1,476/s (both averaged while messages were
    flowing).
  - Leaderboard: 1,545 updates reached the 10 subscribers.
  - Load distribution: exactly 25% per server (12,500 POSTs each).
  - CPU and memory:

  ┌────────────────┬─────────┬─────────┬────────────┐
  │      Host      │ Avg CPU │ Max CPU │ Max memory │
  ├────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────────┤
  │ ingest servers │ 11–15%  │ 27–39%  │ about 45%  │
  ├────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────────┤
  │ consumer       │ 9%      │ —       │ 41%        │
  ├────────────────┼─────────┼─────────┼────────────┤
  │ RabbitMQ       │ 40%     │ 74%     │ 57%        │
  └────────────────┴─────────┴─────────┴────────────┘

    CPU steal was at most 1.2%, so no host was throttled for running out of CPU
  credits.

  Three things I noticed

  1. The client location limits the results. Each request takes about 26 ms
     because it crosses the internet from this Mac, while the lag through the
     system is only 3 ms. 64 threads therefore can't exceed about 2,500
     swipes/s; the measured 1,498/s includes the 32-thread warmup. Rounds 1–2
     will mostly measure the network path from this Mac, not the servers.
     Running the client on an EC2 instance in us-west-2 avoids that, but
     launching one is an infrastructure change.
  2. RabbitMQ is the busiest machine. It peaked at 74% CPU at only about 1,500
     msg/s on a t3.micro. It's the likely bottleneck at higher rates, which is
     useful to know for the 1M stress test.
  3. The lag measurement covers about 87% of main-phase messages (15,627 of
     18,000). By design it skips the first one to two seconds of the main phase,
     so no warmup message can be counted. Message coverage is checked
     separately: 0 missing headers.

  Current state

  - The consumer is at the baseline (N=10, prefetch 100, ACK 1). The SSH tunnels
    are still open.
  - Results are in results/tuning/smoke-c064-k10/ (summary, CSVs,
    queue_depth.svg).
  - The original configuration is still to be restored, after the last round.

  Decision before Round 1:
  - (a) Run Rounds 1–6 from this Mac as planned, and state the network-latency
    limit in the report.
  - (b) I launch a temporary t3.small client instance in us-west-2 (an
    infrastructure change), run the rounds from there, and terminate it
    afterwards. This gives client-thread and throughput results that reflect the
    system rather than the internet path. One complication: the AWS CLI is
    currently denied (voc-cancel-cred), so for (b) you'd have to either refresh
    the credentials or launch the instance yourself.

  Which do you want?

✻ Sautéed for 9s · done Sunday 7:34 PM

❯ check the r1-c064 queue depth chart                                           

⏺ I'll look at both the raw series and the chart itself:

  Read 1 file, ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The chart shows a single isolated spike while the rest of the run stays near
  zero. I'm now checking the lag maximum and host CPU around t ≈ 50 s to tell a
  consumer stall from a statistics burst.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ That spike is a real event, not a sampling glitch: a very short delivery stall
  on RabbitMQ, which is running near 100% CPU. The automatic label plateau on
  the chart is wrong, though. The queue was flat apart from one short spike.

  What the chart and data show
  - The queue stayed tiny. Depth was 13–38 messages for the whole run, apart
    from one sample: 865 at t = 50.8 s. The samples on either side read 15
    and 24. Publishing and ACKs both stayed steady at about 4.7–4.8K/s right
    through it.
  - It lasted well under one 5 s sample. 865 messages is about 180 ms of traffic
    at 4.7K/s. The measured lag agrees: max 156 ms, while p99 stayed at 26 ms.
    So for roughly 150–180 ms, messages piled up and then cleared before the
    next sample.
  - The consumer isn't the cause. Its CPU was only 13% at that moment, and the
    client and servers were steady.
  - RabbitMQ is. It ran at 97–99% CPU from 44 to 54 s. At about 4.7K msg/s, the
    t3.micro broker is close to saturation, and a brief stall there (garbage
    collection, statistics collection, disk writes for persistent messages)
    makes messages pile up for a fraction of a second.

  What it means for the tuning
  - Targets: the run still passes. Max depth 865 is under 1000, lag p99 is 26
    ms, and nothing was lost. But the margin to 1000 is small, and only because
    the spike was short.
  - Bottleneck: RabbitMQ CPU, not the consumer, is the limit. Higher client
    thread counts push more messages through it, so bigger spikes, or a sawtooth
    pattern, are likely in the 256- and 512-thread runs. That's useful evidence
    for the "find bottlenecks" part of the report.
  - Sampling limit: a stall shorter than 5 s can be missed between samples, so
    other runs may have had similar spikes that weren't recorded. The measured
    lag maximum catches them instead, which makes it a useful companion to max
    depth.

  The labeling problem: queue_depth_profile called this a "plateau" because the
  depth crossed 50% of the peak and fell back only once. But a plateau means
  depth stays high for a sustained period, which this wasn't. I'd like to
  tighten the rule:
  - plateau only if at least half of the load-window samples are above 50% of
    the peak;
  - sawtooth stays as it is (at least two rise-and-fall cycles);
  - anything else is flat, with spike added when a single sample rises above 50%
    of the peak (this run would become flat+spike).

  That's a change to the monitoring script only; it doesn't affect any measured
  number. I'd recompute the labels for the runs already finished. Should I make
  it? Round 1 keeps running either way; this doesn't need a pause.

  Notes:
  - r1-c064 is now flat+spike, which matches the chart: one short stall during a
    run that otherwise stayed near zero.
  - r1-c256 is a real sawtooth (265 → 27 → 138 → 0), but at low depth, far
    below 1000. It still passes all three targets, with measured lag p99 50 ms.
    It's the first sign of the oscillation that RabbitMQ's CPU limit should make
    more visible at higher load.
  - One weakness of the rule, for your decision:
    - The plateau test counts every sample in the window, including the idle
      seconds at the start (JVM start, subscriber connect) and the drain at the
      end. That's why r1-c128 comes out as flat, with 6 of 13 samples above half
      its peak.
    - Counting only the samples taken while messages were being published, it
      would be a plateau, with 6 of 9 samples. Its depth did hold steady at
      about 40–80 throughout the load.
    - I'd count only the publishing samples, which better matches what "plateau"
      means. That changes only the plateau test. Should I change it, or keep
      the rule as approved?
