  description of the summary fields is also out of date (it still mentions
  meets_queue_target and queue_depth_cycles); I'll update it at the end,
  together with the final results.

⏺ Round 1 found 256 client threads gave the best throughput (5,447/s) with no
  failures or loss, while 512 threads saturated and broke the lag check.
  RabbitMQ, not the consumer, is the bottleneck. Next I'm running Round 2,
  testing 10, 20, 40 and 80 consumer threads at 256 client threads, all back to
  back.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

  run "flat" (add "+spike" if applicable) regardless of shape; use              
  plateau/sawtooth only when the peak is at least 200. Then freeze the profile  
  rules and relabel all finished runs. No more rule changes unless I ask.       

⏺ I'm applying the new rule and locking it in. Measurements will use the
  publishing peak, plateau counts only publishing samples, sawtooth counts
  cycles over the whole load window, and "+spike" requires a publishing peak of
  at least 50.

  Why 256 won over 512

  By the approved rule:
  1. 512 doesn't qualify. Its measured lag p99 is 104 ms, over the 100 ms
     target, so it fails the "all three targets PASS" requirement.
  2. Among the qualifying runs (64, 128, 256), 256 has the highest throughput:
     5,447/s, 12% above 128. That's well outside the 5% tie margin, so no
     tie-break was needed.

  And 512 would lose even without the lag target. Its throughput is lower than
  256's (5,364 vs 5,447/s) while every latency figure roughly doubles: mean 71
  vs 36 ms, p99 182 vs 91 ms, lag p99 104 vs 50 ms. Past 256 threads, adding
  client concurrency gets no more work through; the extra requests just wait
  longer.

  Why the system saturates there:
  - RabbitMQ is the limit. It peaked at 99.9% CPU in both the 256 and 512 runs,
    while the consumer stayed at about 14% and the ingest servers at about
    13–14%.
  - Gains shrink as the broker fills up. 64 → 128 threads gave +17% throughput,
    128 → 256 only +12%, and 256 → 512 −1.5%. At 512, the extra in-flight
    requests queue in Tomcat and the servers' publisher pools, and messages wait
    longer in RabbitMQ. That shows up as higher latency, a higher sawtooth peak
    (422), and the lag-target failure.
  - Implication for Round 2: the bottleneck is the broker, not the consumer, so
    adding consumer threads shouldn't help either. Single-active-consumer also
    limits the queues to 10 active consumers.

⏺ The ready/unacked split is now recorded and reported. The prefetch-250 run,
  which hasn't started yet, will be the first to include it.

  Answer to your depth question:
  - The target uses total depth. depth is RabbitMQ's messages field (ready +
    unacked) summed over the 10 resort queues, the same "Total" the console
    shows. It stays the official measure for < 1000.
  - The split is now recorded too. queue.csv has ready and unacked columns, and
    each run's summary and the comparison include queue_ready_max (real backlog)
    and queue_unacked_max (messages prefetched to consumers). Runs recorded
    before this change show these as empty rather than guessed values.
  - One caveat: the maximum ready and maximum unacked can come from different
    samples, so they don't necessarily add up to the maximum total.

  Round 3 so far:

  Run: r3-p010
  Status: Fails depth and lag: total depth max 7,818, measured lag p99 2,819 ms,

  loss PASS, 5,230 swipes/s. The Mac slept during the run (19:47:20–19:56:20),
  so its queue series stops at 40.6 s. The depth max and lag are real, but the
  series is incomplete, and the run predates the ready/unacked split.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Run: r3-p100
  Status: running now (started before the split was added)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Run: r3-p250
  Status: next; will include the split

  Why prefetch 10 fails: with one active consumer per queue and at most 10
  messages in flight, each consumer spends much of its time waiting for ACKs to
  travel to RabbitMQ and back, and it can't keep up with about 6,500 msg/s of
  publishing. Depth grew from 118 to 7,818 within 15 s while the ACK rate lagged
  the publish rate. This should show up as ready (real backlog), not unacked;
  at prefetch 10, unacked can't exceed 10 × 10 = 100. The rerun will confirm it.

  What I'll do when Round 3 finishes (follows directly from your request; no new
  decision):
  1. Move the original r3-p010 and r3-p100 run folders to
     results/tuning/_superseded/. They're kept, but no longer counted in the
     comparison.
  2. Rerun both with the same labels, with sleep prevention, so every Round 3
     run has a complete series and the ready/unacked split.
  3. Compare Round 3, then continue to Round 4.

⏺ The reruns came back clean, with 16 samples each and no gaps, and both include
  the ready/unacked split. Next I'm checking the prefetch-10 depth series,
  since its flat+spike label looks wrong for a depth of 10,724.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Prefetch 10's series is a steady ramp: ready messages grow from 17 to 10,624
  while unacked stays pinned at its 100 cap (10 consumers × prefetch 10). The
  consumers can't keep up, which is the "bad" case in the assignment. The frozen
  rule labels it flat+spike, because only the last publishing sample (6,308) is
  above half the publishing peak, and a ramp never falls back, so it never
  completes a sawtooth cycle. I've left the label as it is, since the rule is
  frozen.

  Round 3 result: prefetch 100 wins

  256 client threads, 10 consumer threads, ACK batch 1, pool 64, 232,000 swipes
  each. All runs: 0 failed swipes, 0 loss.

  Prefetch: 10
  Throughput: 5,144/s
  Latency p95 / p99: 67 / 107 ms
  Total depth max: 10,724
  Ready max / unacked max: 10,624 / 100
  Lag p50 / p99 / max (measured): 122 / 1,739 / 1,954 ms
  Depth / lag / loss: FAIL / FAIL / PASS
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Prefetch: 100
  Throughput: 5,235/s
  Latency p95 / p99: 69 / 106 ms
  Total depth max: 175
  Ready max / unacked max: 0 / 175
  Lag p50 / p99 / max (measured): 16 / 58 / 181 ms
  Depth / lag / loss: PASS / PASS / PASS
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Prefetch: 250
  Throughput: 5,051/s
  Latency p95 / p99: 79 / 150 ms
  Total depth max: 143
  Ready max / unacked max: 0 / 143
  Lag p50 / p99 / max (measured): 17 / 59 / 440 ms
  Depth / lag / loss: PASS / PASS / PASS

  Why 100 wins: prefetch 10 fails depth and lag. Of the two qualifying runs, 100
  has the higher throughput. 250 is 3.5% lower, inside the 5% tie margin, so
  the tie-break applies, and 100 also has the lower latency p99 (106 vs 150 ms)
  and lag max (181 vs 440 ms).

  What the ready/unacked split shows:
  - Prefetch 10: almost all of the depth is real backlog (ready). Each consumer
    can hold only 10 messages and waits for ACK round-trips, so it falls behind
    at 6,000+ msg/s.
  - Prefetch 100 and 250: ready never rises above 0, and all of the depth is
    prefetched messages sitting with the consumers, so there's no real backlog
    at all. The 1000 target, measured on total depth, isn't threatened. With
    prefetch 250 the unacked messages could in principle reach 2,500, but only
    143 were ever in flight.
  - Bottleneck: RabbitMQ again peaked at 98–100% CPU in every run, while the
    consumer stayed at about 15%.

  One note on the rules: the approved plan said the baseline should win ties in
  Rounds 3–5, but summarize.py implements "lower p99 latency" as the tie-break
  there. In this round both give the same answer (100). If the two ever disagree
  in Round 4 or 5, I'll stop and ask.

  Round 4: batch ACK 1 / 10 / 50, at prefetch 100 (both batch sizes are at most
  the prefetch), 256 client threads, 10 consumer threads, pool 64. Labels
  r4-c256-k10-a001, -a010, -a050. Running with sleep prevention:

  Where the rules split:
  - Tie margin: 64 is fastest. 128 is 1.5% slower, inside the 5% margin. 32 is
    5.2% slower, just outside it, so it's out.
  - Implemented tie-break (summarize.py --compare): lowest latency p99 picks
    128, at 97.9 vs 100.4 ms.
  - Approved plan: in Rounds 3–5 "the baseline wins a tie", which picks 64.

  My recommendation: 64.
  - Throughput is slightly higher, and the latency and lag differences (2.5 ms
    at p99, 1 ms at lag p99) are well within run-to-run noise. Earlier repeats
    of the same configuration varied by about 1–3%.
  - It's the existing default, so the final configuration needs no server
    change.
  - It's the rule you approved. The latency tie-break in the script is an
    implementation detail that differs from the plan.

  What the round shows either way: 32 channels are slightly too few. Throughput
  drops about 5%, and lag p50 rises from 18 to 24 ms because requests wait for a
  free channel. Going from 64 to 128 adds nothing, because RabbitMQ is still at
  99–100% CPU. Server CPU stayed at about 22–25% in every run.

  Current state:
  - all 4 servers are running with pool 128 (from the last run);
  - the consumer is at 10 threads, prefetch 100, ACK 1;
  - the queues are drained;
  - the original setenv.sh files are backed up as setenv.sh.orig-20261102.

         -lateau, several is a sawtooth), producer rate, consumer rate, t
         -ime to drain after the client finishes.

         -y if nothing is missing, nothing went to the DLQ, and the queue
         -s drained.                                                     
      40 +- **Queue:** sampled every 5 s, RabbitMQ's statistics interval.
         + A spike shorter than one sample can be missed.                
      41 +  - `queue_depth_max` and `queue_depth_p95` use total depth (re
         +ady + unacked, as the RabbitMQ console shows it). `queue_ready_
         +max` is real backlog; `queue_unacked_max` is messages prefetche
         +d to consumers.                                                
      42 +  - `queue_depth_profile` (frozen rule; see `depth_profile` in 
         +`summarize.py`):                                               
      43 +    - `flat` when the publishing peak is below 200;            
      44 +    - otherwise `sawtooth` (at least two rise-and-fall cycles) 
         +or `plateau` (at least half of the publishing samples above 50%
         + of the peak);                                                 
      45 +    - otherwise `flat`; `+spike` is added when exactly one publ
         +ishing sample is above half a peak of at least 50.             
      46 +  - Producer and consumer rates are averaged over samples where
         + messages were flowing. `queue_depth.svg` plots depth over time
         +.                                                              
      47 +- **Consumer lag (measured):** `lag_p50_ms`, `lag_p99_ms` and `
         +lag_max_ms` come from the consumer's `/lag` endpoint. Lag is pr
         +ocessing start minus the ingest server's `x-publish-ms` publish
         + time, for messages published in the main phase only; the warmu
         +p is excluded by publish time. `est_drain_*_ms` (depth ÷ ack ra
         +te) is only an estimate of backlog drain time and is never used
         + as lag.                                                       
      48 +- **Swipe loss:** `swipes_missing` = client successes − swipes 
         +the consumer processed during the run. `loss_check` is PASS onl
         +y if nothing is missing, nothing went to the DLQ, and the queue
         +s drained. `consumer_retries` and `consumer_ack_failures` are t
         +he consumer's own counters.

      50 +  - `target_depth`: `queue_depth_max` < 1000;                  
      51 +  - `target_lag`: `lag_p99_ms` < 100, or NOT MEASURED without s
         +amples or if more than 1% of messages had no publish header;   
      52 +  - `target_loss`: `loss_check` PASS;                          
      53 +  - `targets_overall` is PASS only if all three pass.

      57  ## Part 4 tuning
      58  
      59  Every tuning run uses the same conditions: 4 ingest servers beh
          ind the ALB, `--swipes 232000` (200K plus the 32K warmup), pref
          etch 100, batch ACK 1, channel pool 64, and single-active-consu
          mer resort queues.

⏺ Part 4 tuning is done. All five rounds and the final validation ran on AWS.
  The chosen configuration (256 client threads, 10 consumer threads, prefetch
  100, ACK batch 1, pool 64) passed all three targets in the final validation 
  run, with autoscaling on. The original configuration is restored everywhere.

  How the runs were set up

  Every run:
  - Workload: 232,000 swipes, sent from an EC2 client in us-west-2 through the
    ALB to 4 ingest servers.
  - Queues: single-active-consumer on, 10 resort queues, Top-N 20.
  - Data: everything below is real AWS measurement.
  - Overall: 0 failed swipes and 0 loss in every one of the 20 runs.

  Results by round

  Round: 1 Client threads
  Values tested: 64 / 128 / 256 / 512
  Winner: 256 (5,447/s)
  Why: 512 is slower (5,364/s), its latency doubles, and its measured lag p99 is

  104 ms, failing the target. 256 is 12% faster than 128.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Round: 2 Consumer threads
  Values tested: 10 / 20 / 40 / 80
  Winner: 10 (5,420/s)
  Why: All within 3%, so the fewest threads win. RabbitMQ showed 1 active + 7
  standby consumers per queue at 80 threads, so the extra threads only add
  failover.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Round: 3 Prefetch
  Values tested: 10 / 100 / 250
  Winner: 100 (5,235/s)
  Why: Prefetch 10 fails depth and lag: ready backlog reached 10,624 and lag p99

  1,739 ms. At 100 and 250 ready stayed at 0; 250 had higher latency p99 (150
  vs 106 ms) and lag max (440 vs 181 ms).
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Round: 4 Batch ACK
  Values tested: 1 / 10 / 50
  Winner: 1
  Why: All within 5% (5,144–5,391/s); both tie-break rules pick 1. Batching gave

  about 2% per step, within noise.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Round: 5 Channel pool
  Values tested: 32 / 64 / 128
  Winner: 64 (your call)
  Why: 32 is 5% slower, and lag p50 rises from 18 to 24 ms. 64 and 128 tie. You
  chose 64 per the approved rule; the script's latency tie-break prefers 128.

  Final validation: the chosen configuration with dynamic scaling on

  256 client threads · consumer start 10, min 5, max 10, autoscaling on ·
  prefetch 100 · ACK 1 · pool 64

  ┌──────────────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  Thread count over time is saved in consumer_threads.csv in the run folder:
  - Scaled down: 10 → 9 → 8 → 7 while idle before the load, then 7 → 6 → 5 in
    the first 7 s of load.
  - Never scaled up: the autoscaler adds a thread only when the ready backlog
    exceeds 2,000 × threads (10,000 at 5 threads), and ready stayed at 0
    throughout. 5 threads, each serving two resort queues, kept up with about
    7,000 msg/s.

  So the run shows scaling down. Showing scaling up needs real backlog, for
  example a lower CONSUMER_SCALE_UP_BACKLOG_PER_THREAD or a deliberately
  constrained run. That would be a new experiment, so I haven't run one; tell me
  if you want it.

  summarize.py --compare prints "best: none" for the final group. That's only
  because the thread count changed during the run, which counts as "not
  confirmed" for tuning runs. The run itself passed all three targets.

  Findings for the report

  - The bottleneck is RabbitMQ. The t3.micro broker hit 98–100% CPU in every
    tuning run above 64 client threads. The consumer stayed at about 14% and the
    ingest servers at about 13–25%. That's why consumer threads, batch ACK and
    pool size barely matter here.
  - Queue profiles: with prefetch ≥ 100 the depth is only prefetched messages,
    with no real backlog. Prefetch 10 produces the "bad" pattern from the
    assignment: depth ramps up because consumers can't keep up. The frozen rule
    labels that run flat+spike, since a ramp never completes a cycle, so
    describe its shape in the report rather than relying on the label.

  Restored state (all verified)

  - consumer.env: identical to the original (cmp). The consumer runs its
    defaults: start 5, min 1, max 10, autoscaling on.
  - setenv.sh on all 4 servers: identical to the original (cmp), 64 channels
    each in RabbitMQ.
  - New builds kept, as you asked:
    - WAR 94054a2f… on all servers (old one in ~/ski-servlet.war.bak-20261102);
    - consumer JAR abab111b… (old one in /opt/skitrak/…jar.bak-20261102).
  - Queues and housekeeping: drained, DLQ empty, SSH tunnels closed.

  Files

  client.log, queue.csv with ready/unacked, queue_depth.svg, hosts/*.csv,
  distribution.csv, the client's per-swipe CSV), plus summary.csv and

⏺ Yes, with the same tools as the tuning runs and no script changes needed. A
  few things need your decision first.

  What gets reused

  - run_test.py runs each test: the client on the EC2 client instance over SSH
    (the Assignment 1 client-part2, which does both ingest and subscribe), the
    queue monitor, host monitors on all hosts, the queue-drain wait, and the
    before/after counters.
  - summarize.py writes each run's summary.json and the combined summary.csv.
    Every required metric is already there:
    - Client: runtime, throughput, failures, retries, latency.
    - Queue: peak and average depth (plus ready/unacked), producer rate,
      consumer rate.
    - System: CPU, memory and network for every host.
    - Distribution: POSTs per ingest server, from the Tomcat access logs.
    - Also reported: measured lag and the three target results.
  - The safety steps stay the same: drain checks, the DLQ check, sleep
    prevention, the SSH tunnels, and never purging.

  The labels would be perf-1srv-500k, perf-2srv-500k, perf-4srv-500k and
  perf-4srv-1m. I'd compare them through summary.csv/comparison.csv; the tuning
  "winner" logic doesn't apply here.

  What I need you to decide or do

  1. Swipe counts. The client's total includes its 32K warmup, and its built-in
     default is 532000. That suggests "500K" means --swipes 532000 (500K main +
     warmup) and the stress test 1032000. Or did you mean 500K in total?
  2. Configuration. I recommend the tuned, validated configuration:
     - Client: 256 threads. The client's own default is 400 threads.
     - Consumer: start 10, min 5, max 10, autoscaling on, prefetch 100, ACK 1.
     - Servers: pool 64.

     That means setting the consumer keys again (and restoring the originals
     afterwards). The alternative is the restored original defaults: start 5,
     min 1, max 10.
  3. 1 and 2 servers, which you'll have to set up. Those tests need servers
     removed from the ALB target group, and the AWS API is still blocked for me
     (voc-cancel-cred). Going straight to one server's port 8080 isn't an option
     either: the security group only allows 8080 from the ALB and your IP, not
     from the client instance.
     - What you'd do: deregister targets in the console (for example server2–4
       for the 1-server test, server3–4 for the 2-server test), then re-register
       them.
     - How I check it: the per-server POST counts must show traffic only on the
       registered servers. If not, the run is flagged and I stop.
  4. Leaderboard confirmation is still only half covered. The scripts show that
     subscribers receive real updates (update counts, and the server no longer
     makes up synthetic boards). They don't show that the contents match the
     swipes, because the client throws away the board data. Two options:
     - (a) Manual check: once, with no load running, post 2 swipes for a fresh
       skier on an unused day and capture the pushed board (397 → 741 vertical).
       That needs no code change.
     - (b) Client change: keep the last board per subscriber and compare it with
       totals computed from the per-swipe CSV. The client currently doesn't
       record skier IDs or lifts, so this is a bigger change.

     I recommend (a).

  Risks for the 1M run

  - Message limits: each queue holds at most 10,000 messages with a 60 s TTL. If
    RabbitMQ (already at 99–100% CPU) falls behind long enough, messages get
    dropped into the dead-letter queue. The loss check would catch that, and
    it's itself a bottleneck finding.
  - CPU credits: 1M swipes take about 3–4 minutes on t3 instances. CPU steal is
    recorded, so throttling will be visible.
  - Client memory: 1M records fit in the client JVM's default heap on the
    t3.micro (roughly 80 MB needed). The client instance's CPU and memory are
    monitored too.

  Answer points 1, 2 and 4, and let me know when the target group is set for the
  1-server run. Nothing starts until then.

       1 +import java.net.URI;                                           
       2 +import java.net.http.HttpClient;                               
       3 +import java.net.http.WebSocket;                                
       4 +import java.time.Instant;                                      
       5 +import java.util.concurrent.CompletionStage;                   
       6 +                                                               
       7 +// Usage: java Sub.java <ws-url> <seconds>. Prints every WebSoc
         +ket message with its receive time (UTC).                       
       8 +public class Sub {                                             
       9 +    public static void main(String[] a) throws Exception {     
      10 +        StringBuilder buf = new StringBuilder();               
      11 +        WebSocket ws = HttpClient.newHttpClient().newWebSocketB
         +uilder().buildAsync(URI.create(a[0]), new WebSocket.Listener() 
         +{                                                              
      12 +            public void onOpen(WebSocket ws) {                 
      13 +                System.out.println(Instant.now() + " OPEN " + a
         +[0]);                                                          
      14 +                ws.request(1);                                 
      15 +            }                                                  
      16 +            public CompletionStage<?> onText(WebSocket ws, Char
         +Sequence data, boolean last) {                                 
      17 +                buf.append(data);                              
      18 +                if (last) {                                    
      19 +                    System.out.println(Instant.now() + " MSG " 
         ++ buf);                                                        
      20 +                    buf.setLength(0);                          
      21 +                }                                              
      22 +                ws.request(1);                                 
      23 +                return null;                                   
      24 +            }                                                  
      25 +            public CompletionStage<?> onClose(WebSocket ws, int
         + code, String reason) {                                        
      26 +                System.out.println(Instant.now() + " CLOSE " + 
         +code + " " + reason);                                          
      27 +                return null;                                   
      28 +            }                                                  
      29 +        }).join();                                             
      30 +        // The ALB closes idle connections after 120 s; a ping 
         +every 30 s keeps this one open.                                
      31 +        long end = System.currentTimeMillis() + Long.parseLong(
         +a[1]) * 1000;                                                  
      32 +        while (System.currentTimeMillis() < end) {             
      33 +            Thread.sleep(30_000);                              
      34 +            ws.sendPing(java.nio.ByteBuffer.allocate(0));      
      35 +        }                                                      
      36 +        System.out.println(Instant.now() + " END");            
      37 +    }                                                          
      38 +}

  Notes

  - The lag failure is real, but not a queue backlog. Ready stayed at 0, so
    RabbitMQ was never holding a backlog; messages sat at most as prefetched (≤
    95 unacked). The extra lag builds up between the publish time on server1 and
    the start of processing on the consumer. Two candidates:
    - RabbitMQ averaged 88% CPU, its highest average so far (about 70% in the
      4-server tuning runs). It had to handle all 532K messages over a single
      publisher connection.
    - The consumer ran at only 5 threads (MIN) for the whole run, each serving 2
      queues, because the autoscaler scales up only on ready backlog, which
      never appeared.

    Which of the two dominates will show more clearly in the 2- and 4-server
    runs. Throughput about matched the 4-server tuning results (≈5.4K/s), so a
    single server isn't limiting throughput.
  - The client isn't the bottleneck yet, but it's close. It averaged 64% CPU and
    peaked at 91%, with 9% steal. I'll watch this especially in the 1M run.
  - The ALB idle timeout didn't affect subscribers: 0 drops and 0 reconnects,
    because updates keep the connections busy.

  The tuned consumer configuration stays applied between runs, and is re-applied
  with a fresh restart before each one. The originals get restored after the
  last run.

  What this shows

  - Load balancing works. The split was exactly 50 / 50, and each server's CPU
    dropped from 48% to about 35%. Throughput rose 17.5%, and latency and lag
    p99 roughly halved. That's less than a 2× gain because RabbitMQ is still at
    85–100% CPU.
  - The depth target failed only because of prefetched messages. Total depth
    peaked at 1,054, but 994 of those were unacked (messages already delivered
    to consumers) and only 60 were ready. With 5 threads each serving 2 queues,
    the unacked limit is 5 × 2 × 100 = 1,000, and the faster flow filled it
    once. By the official measure (total depth, as you decided) it's a FAIL.
    Real backlog stayed at 60.
  - Lag still fails, but it's half the 1-server value (p99 129 vs 246 ms). The
    consumer again stayed at MIN = 5 threads, because the autoscaler only scales
    up on ready backlog of more than 10,000.
  - ⚠️ The client is getting close to being a bottleneck. CPU averaged 67% with
    peaks at 92%, and steal reached 33%, meaning the hypervisor throttled the
    t3.micro for part of the run, probably because it ran out of CPU credits. It
    didn't stop throughput from rising, but the 4-server and 1M runs could be
    limited by the client rather than the system. I'll flag it in those results
    if it shows.

  probes that all four servers receive traffic, then run perf-4srv-532k,
  followed by perf-4srv-1032k after your go-ahead.

⏺ That narrows it down. One more check: RabbitMQ's log for the whole window,
  apart from the routine logins:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The likely cause is a brief stall inside RabbitMQ. At ~99% CPU, the broker
  fell behind on processing ACKs for roughly 150–200 ms. It was not a consumer
  pause or an autoscaler resize. A rerun isn't needed to explain it, but more
  detail on that below.

  What the data shows at 06:09:24 (RabbitMQ's 5 s statistics sample; the
  console's ~06:09:30 is the same event):
  - Total depth went 55 → 1,054 → 82. Of those 1,054, 994 were unacked and only
    60 ready.
  - 994 is almost exactly the unacked ceiling. At that moment the consumer ran 5
    threads, each serving 2 queues with prefetch 100, which gives 5 × 2 × 100 =
    1,000. So for one sample, RabbitMQ counted almost every possible in-flight
    message as delivered but not yet acknowledged.

  What it wasn't:

  Suspect: Consumer resize
  Evidence: The last resize (6 → 5) was at 06:08:54, 30 s earlier; none after
  Verdict: ❌ ruled out
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Suspect: Consumer GC or JVM pause
  Evidence: Its 10-second status lines ran at 10.025 / 10.015 / 10.002 / 10.005
  s
  intervals, so it never froze for more than about 25 ms. It processed a steady
   ~6.4K swipes/s in every 10 s window (64,044 / 64,307 / 64,660 / 64,778).
  Consumer CPU was flat at 14–18%, with no steal
  Verdict: ❌ ruled out
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Suspect: Consumer falling behind
  Evidence: The per-second lag histogram shows no second with lag over 212 ms,
  and the message count per publish second stayed steady (6.0–6.8K). A pause of
   a second or more would show up as lags over 1,000 ms
  Verdict: ❌ ruled out
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Suspect: Client, servers or host throttling
  Evidence: Client CPU 72–76%, servers about 40%, steal ≈ 0 on every host at
  that
  moment; nothing in the servers' catalina.out
  Verdict: ❌ ruled out
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Suspect: RabbitMQ alarm or flow control
  Evidence: No memory or disk alarms, and nothing in the broker log apart from
  routine logins
  Verdict: ❌ none recorded
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Suspect: RabbitMQ saturation
  Evidence: 97–100% CPU from 06:09:07 onward, the highest load of any run.
  Messages published at 06:09:22 were the only ones with an unusual lag (p99
  171 ms, vs. 60–97 ms in the neighboring seconds)
  Verdict: ✅ most likely

  What probably happened:
  1. The consumer processed and acknowledged messages at a steady rate
     throughout.
  2. For about 150–200 ms, RabbitMQ was late processing incoming ACKs and
     handing out new deliveries. At 99% CPU, a short scheduling delay inside the
     broker is enough; plausible triggers are its own garbage collection, its
     statistics collection, or writing persistent messages to disk. The logs
     can't tell which.
  3. Unacked therefore briefly piled up to the 1,000 cap, about 60 messages
     waited as ready, and the console showed a short dip in the rates.
  4. It cleared within the next 5 s sample.

  r1-c064 showed the same pattern earlier (one sample at 865, while RabbitMQ was
  at 97–99% CPU).

  On rerunning:
  - It's not an error in the run. It's a real effect of the saturated broker,
    and an example of the "find bottlenecks" evidence the report needs.
  - The depth-target failure is real by the official measure (total depth
    1,054), but the real backlog was only 60 ready messages.
  - A rerun would only test repeatability. Since these stalls are short and
    random, a rerun might pass or fail on depth by chance. I'd keep this run as
    the official result and explain the spike in the report. If you want one
    rerun to show whether it recurs, I'd add it as a second 2-server data point
    rather than replacing this one.

  Your call: keep this run as is or add a repeat run. Either way, the next step
  after that is registering server3 and server4 for the 4-server runs.

  Notes

  - MIN=10 kept 10 consumers active the whole run, and it never scaled up (ready
    stayed at 0, so there was no backlog to trigger it). Depth stayed low
    (max 104), with no repeat of the 1,054 spike, so the depth target now
    passes.
  - Lag still fails (p99 169 ms), and more consumer threads didn't fix it. p99
    is about the same as with 5 threads (129 ms), while the worst case improved
    (max 516 vs 921 ms). Since ready is 0, the extra lag isn't consumers falling
    behind; it builds up inside the saturated broker, which ran at 82% average
    and 99.9% peak CPU.
  - ⚠️ The client is now borderline. Its CPU peaked at 97%, and steal still
    reached 18% despite unlimited credits (that steal comes from the hypervisor,
    not credit throttling). Throughput was 1.6% lower than the MIN=5 run,
    within noise, so it isn't clearly limiting yet. In the 4-server and 1M runs,
    it's the first thing I'll check before calling anything a system
    bottleneck.

  running perf-1srv-532k-min10.

  Notes

  - The MIN=10 and MIN=5 results are nearly identical (throughput −0.1%, lag p99
    240 vs 246 ms). With one server, the number of consumer threads makes no
    measurable difference, which again points to the bottleneck being outside
    the consumer.
  - Lag with 1 server (p99 240 ms) is clearly worse than with 2 (169 ms), even
    though ready stays at 0 and the consumer isn't busy. RabbitMQ runs hotter
    here (88% average CPU, vs 82% with 2 servers) while handling less
    throughput.
    - A plausible explanation: all publishes arrive over one AMQP connection
      from server1, with a publisher confirm for each message. RabbitMQ handles
      each connection's traffic in a single internal process and can slow a busy
      connection down with its built-in flow control, so messages wait inside
      the broker before being queued.
    - Not verified: I didn't record the connections' flow state, so this is a
      hypothesis.
  - The client isn't the bottleneck here. It averaged 66% CPU (peaks 93%), and
    throughput matches the MIN=5 run exactly, so the limit comes from the
    1-server path.

  Next: register server2, server3 and server4 (all four servers) and tell me.
  I'll probe that all four receive traffic before perf-4srv-532k-min10. The 1M
  run waits for your separate confirmation after that.

⏺ I'll write the connection-state sampler first. It runs on the consumer 
  instance:
  - It reads the RabbitMQ credentials there, so the password never leaves that
    machine.
  - Every 5 s it records the state (running / flow / blocked), channel count and
    receive rate of each server's publisher connection, plus the consumer's
    connection.
  - It's independent of the SSH tunnels, which the run script reopens at its
    start.

  One limit up front: RabbitMQ refreshes connection statistics every 5 s, so
  state is a snapshot at each refresh. A connection that switches in and out of
  flow quickly is only caught if it happens to be in flow at that moment.

  What it shows

  - Adding servers helps less and less: +16% from 1 to 2 servers, but only +7%
    from 2 to 4. The servers themselves are far from busy at 19% CPU with 4
    servers. The limits sit elsewhere.
  - RabbitMQ is the main bottleneck, at 99.9% CPU peaks regardless of server
    count. Lag improves with more servers but stays above 100 ms.
  - ⚠️ The client is now a co-limit. It peaked at 96% CPU with 72% average. The
    2-to-4 gain is probably capped partly by the t3.micro client, not just the
    system, and I'll note that in the results.
  - On the flow-control hypothesis: with 4 connections, each carrying about a
    quarter of the traffic, no connection was ever in flow. That fits the
    hypothesis, which predicts flow mainly when one connection carries
    everything, but it doesn't prove it. The 1-server flow check after the 1M
    run is the real test. Keep in mind that each sample is a snapshot taken
    every 5 s, so brief flow periods can be missed.

  Next: perf-4srv-1032k-min10 (1,032,000 swipes) on all 4 servers. It won't 
  start until you confirm. For that run I'll pay particular attention to the
  client's CPU and steal, the queue length and TTL limits (10,000 messages and
  60 s per queue), and the connection states.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The consumer's JVM is running with a 230 MB maximum heap (MaxHeapSize
  241,172,480 bytes) and the Serial garbage collector (UseSerialGC true). On a
  2-vCPU, 913 MB machine, Java picks both of these automatically. The JDK there
  has no jcmd, so I can't read the heap directly; the process's resident memory
  is 305 MB, consistent with a full heap. That completes the picture. 1M-run
  results are in; please take your RabbitMQ screenshot (the run ended 06:52:59
  UTC).

  Did the autoscaler scale up? Yes, once, and only after the backlog had formed

  ┌─────────────┬───────────┬────────┬──────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Time (UTC)  │   Phase   │ Resize │                Reason                │
  ├─────────────┼───────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 06:49:31    │ startup   │ 0 → 10 │ start = 10                           │
  ├─────────────┼───────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │             │ load,     │ 10 →   │ ready backlog above 2,000 × 10 =     │
  │ 06:52:37.97 │ 161.6 s   │ 11     │ 20,000 (RabbitMQ showed 12,878 ready │
  │             │ in        │        │  at 06:52:35 and 31,359 at 06:52:40) │
  ├─────────────┼───────────┼────────┼──────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ 06:52:51.49 │ drain     │ 11 →   │ backlog below 100 after publishing   │
  │             │           │ 10     │ stopped                              │
  └─────────────┴───────────┴────────┴──────────────────────────────────────┘

  The 11th thread was assigned resort 1 (10=[1] in the log). With
  single-active-consumer it became a standby, so it added no processing
  capacity.

  (1) Redelivered ~200/s around 06:52: yes, caused by the resize

  - What a resize does: stops all workers one after another, closes their
    channels, then starts the new set.
  - The redeliveries are the in-flight messages from that: closing the channels
    returned every unacked message to the queues. RabbitMQ's 5 s history shows
    redeliveries only in the 06:52:40 interval: about 200/s × 5 s ≈ 1,000 
    messages, exactly the unacked limit (10 consumers × prefetch 100).
  - It added to the backlog: during the pause, deliveries fell to 4,268/s and
    ACKs to 3,970/s while publishing stayed at 7,944/s. That's why ready jumped
    from 12,878 to its 31,359 peak.
  - The scale-down at 06:52:51 caused no redeliveries: the queues were already
    almost empty by then.

  (2) Duplicates and final state

  - Duplicates detected and skipped: 0. The redelivered messages had been
    delivered but not yet processed (they were waiting in the workers' local
    buffers when the channels closed), so each was processed exactly once after
    redelivery.
  - Counts: processed 1,032,000 = client successes 1,032,000 · dead-lettered 0 ·
    DLQ 0 · consumer retries 0 · ACK failures 0.
  - Why that's sound:
    - the duplicate check keeps 200,000 event IDs per resort, more than the
      ~103,000 per resort in this run, so any already-applied message would have
      been caught;
    - single-active-consumer, plus the resize stopping all workers before new
      ones start, means no resort was ever processed by two threads at once;
    - the leaderboard guard keeps totals from going backwards.
  - What I can't prove: I can't independently recompute each skier's total. The
    client doesn't record skier, lift or vertical per swipe, and nothing
    captured a copy of the events. The evidence for correctness is exactly-once
    processing (counts match, 0 duplicates, 0 dropped), not a recount. Verifying
    the totals directly would need a rerun that captures the events, for
    example a temporary audit queue on the exchange.

  (3) What triggered the backlog near the end: most likely consumer memory 
  pressure, not RabbitMQ or the resize

  ┌───────────────────┬───────────┬───────────┬────────────────┬───────────┐
  │                   │ RabbitMQ  │ Consumer  │  Lag p50 (by   │           │
  │       Time        │    CPU    │    CPU    │    publish     │   Ready   │
  │                   │           │           │    second)     │           │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────┼───────────┼────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 06:50:25–06:51:45 │ 98–100%   │ 13–19%    │ ~20 ms         │ 0         │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────┼───────────┼────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 06:51:50–06:52:00 │ 95–100%   │ 16–22%    │ 300–433 ms     │ 0         │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────┼───────────┼────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 06:52:05–06:52:20 │ 94–99%    │ 34–42%    │ 250–520 ms     │ 0 → 2,365 │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────┼───────────┼────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 06:52:25–06:52:35 │ 96–99%    │ 43–45%    │ 1.0 → 2.5 →    │ 3,561 →   │
  │                   │           │           │ 5.3 s          │ 12,878    │
  ├───────────────────┼───────────┼───────────┼────────────────┼───────────┤
  │ 06:52:37          │           │           │ 7.7 s (resize  │ → 31,359  │
  │                   │           │           │ happens here)  │           │
  └───────────────────┴───────────┴───────────┴────────────────┴───────────┘

  - RabbitMQ isn't what changed. It ran at 98–100% CPU from 06:50:25 to the end,
    as in every run, with no backlog for the first 2 minutes.
  - The resize came after the backlog: lag had already risen to seconds before
    it.
  - What changed was the consumer. Its CPU tripled, to about 45–52%, while its
    throughput fell. On a 2-vCPU machine that's one core fully busy, which is
    what the single-threaded Serial collector looks like when the heap is nearly
    full and it spends most of its time collecting garbage.
  - Why only near the end: the consumer's in-memory state grows with every
    swipe. A rough estimate:
    - about 1.03M duplicate-check entries (one per event ID) at ~140 B each ≈
      145 MB;
    - about 650K skier totals at ~125 B each ≈ 80 MB;
    - together ≈ 225 MB, roughly the 230 MB heap.

    The 532K runs never got there. The 1M run reached it at about 800K processed
    swipes, matching the onset at 06:51:50.
  - Smaller factors: brief 5-second steal spikes (7–12%) hit all hosts at once
    at 06:51:05 and 06:52:05, a hypervisor event, adding small hiccups (lag p99
    923 ms for messages published at 06:52:01). The client wasn't limiting (CPU
    66–76%, steal about 0 apart from those spikes).

  How sure this is: this is a strong inference, not a confirmed measurement.
  There's no garbage-collection log and no jcmd on the instance. Confirming it
  would mean rerunning the 1M test with GC logging switched on (-Xlog:gc* in the
  consumer's Java options, a configuration change). If confirmed, the options
  are:
  - (a) give the consumer a larger heap (-Xmx512m, which fits in 913 MB);
  - (b) reduce CONSUMER_DEDUP_WINDOW (a 200K window per resort holds up to 2M
    entries);
  - (c) switch to the G1 garbage collector.

  I haven't changed anything. Do you want a confirming rerun with GC logging,
  any of (a)–(c) as a fix, or to keep this as the documented result?

  1M run summary (perf-4srv-1032k-min10)

  Answers

  When the autoscaler resized during the 1M run (UTC):
  - 06:52:37.973: 10 → 11, 161.6 s into the run (the client finished at
    06:52:40). The ready backlog had passed 2,000 × 10 = 20,000. The 11th thread
    got resort 1 and became a single-active-consumer standby, so it added no
    capacity.
  - 06:52:51.490: 11 → 10, in the drain, after the backlog fell below 100.

  What caused redelivered ~200/s:
  - It was the 10 → 11 resize. A resize stops all workers and closes their
    channels, then starts and resubscribes the new set, so every
    delivered-but-unacked message goes back to its queue.
  - Exact count: RabbitMQ's history shows 100 redeliveries on each of the 10 
    queues, 1,000 in total, all in the interval ending 06:52:40. That's 200/s
    over 5 s, and exactly one full prefetch buffer per queue.
  - The resize is visible in the queue data too: unacked fell from its 1,000 cap
    to 400 at the 06:52:38 sample, while delivery and ACK rates dipped (deliver
    4,268/s, ACK 3,970/s, against 7,944/s published).

  Duplicates detected and skipped: 0. The redelivered messages hadn't been
  processed yet, so each was applied exactly once.

  Is the final per-skier state consistent? Yes, by every check available:
  - processed 1,032,000 = client successes 1,032,000, with 0 duplicates, 0
    retries, 0 dead-lettered and 0 ACK failures;
  - the duplicate-check window (200,000 IDs per resort) exceeds the ~103,000
    events per resort, so a re-applied message would have been caught;
  - no resort was ever processed by two threads at once, so order was preserved.

  The totals weren't independently recomputed, though: the client doesn't record
  skier, lift or vertical per swipe.

  Correction to the 1M depth breakdown: the peak total of 31,759 (06:52:38
  sample) was 31,359 ready + 400 unacked. My earlier "31,359 + 1,000" combined
  maxima from different samples; unacked was at its 1,000 cap at other samples.
  The corrected breakdown is in EVIDENCE.md. The summary.json fields
  queue_ready_max and queue_unacked_max stay as they are, since they are
  separate maxima by definition.

  Current state:
  - the consumer is still running with the tuned MIN=10 config;
  - the flow-check run hasn't started; your interruption stopped the probe
    before it ran;
  - the original consumer.env hasn't been restored.

  Should I go ahead with the 1-server flow check (flowcheck-1srv-100k, tagged
  probes first, now that the target group is server1), and then restore the
  original configuration?

  ⎿  Created results/flowcheck/flowcheck-1srv-100k/conn_sampler.py (+26 -0)
       1 +#!/usr/bin/env python3                                         
       2 +"""Samples RabbitMQ connection state (running/flow/blocked) eve
         +ry INTERVAL s into a CSV.                                      
       3 +Read-only: GET /api/connections. Runs on the consumer instance,
         + credentials from consumer.env.                                
       4 +Usage: sudo python3 conn_sampler.py <out.csv> <max seconds> [in
         +terval]"""                                                     
       5 +import base64, csv, json, sys, time, urllib.request            
       6 +out, max_s = sys.argv[1], float(sys.argv[2]); interval = float(
         +sys.argv[3]) if len(sys.argv) > 3 else 5.0                     
       7 +env = dict(l.strip().split("=", 1) for l in open("/etc/skitrak/
         +consumer.env") if "=" in l and not l.startswith("#"))          
       8 +auth = base64.b64encode(f"{env['RABBITMQ_USERNAME']}:{env['RABB
         +ITMQ_PASSWORD']}".encode()).decode()                           
       9 +req = urllib.request.Request(f"http://{env['RABBITMQ_HOST']}:15
         +672/api/connections")                                          
      10 +req.add_header("Authorization", "Basic " + auth)               
      11 +end = time.time() + max_s                                      
      12 +with open(out, "w", newline="") as f:                          
      13 +    w = csv.writer(f); w.writerow(["ts", "peer_host", "name", "
         +state", "channels", "recv_kBps", "send_kBps"])                 
      14 +    while time.time() < end:                                   
      15 +        t = time.time()                                        
      16 +        try:                                                   
      17 +            for c in json.load(urllib.request.urlopen(req, time
         +out=4)):                                                       
      18 +                name = (c.get("client_properties") or {}).get("
         +connection_name", "")                                          
      19 +                if name in ("skitrak-server", "skitrak-consumer
         +"):                                                            
      20 +                    w.writerow([f"{t:.3f}", c.get("peer_host"),
         + name, c.get("state"), c.get("channels"),                      
      21 +                                round(c.get("recv_oct_details",
         + {}).get("rate", 0) / 1024, 1),                                
      22 +                                round(c.get("send_oct_details",
         + {}).get("rate", 0) / 1024, 1)])                               
      23 +            f.flush()                                          
      24 +        except Exception as e:                                 
      25 +            w.writerow([f"{t:.3f}", "", "", f"error: {e}", "", 
         +"", ""])                                                       
      26 +        time.sleep(max(0, interval - (time.time() - t)))

⏺ The flow check is done and everything is restored to its original
  configuration.

  Flow check (flowcheck-1srv-100k): server1 only, 100,000 swipes, sampled every 
  5 s

  - Routing: all 100,000 POSTs landed on server1; servers 2–4 had 0.
  - Connection state: server1's single publisher connection carried 0.8 → 1.7 
    MB/s, about 3× the ~550 KB/s each connection carried in the 4-server run. It
    was running in every sample, never flow. The consumer connection was also
    always running.
  - The lag pattern reproduced: lag p99 219 ms (the full 1-server runs had
    240–246 ms), with ready max 0 and 4,057 swipes/s.
  - CPU: RabbitMQ peaked at 99%; server1 34% average (58% max); consumer 13%;
    client 56% average (92% max).

  What this means for the hypothesis: the evidence doesn't support sustained
  flow control on the single connection as the cause of the higher 1-server lag.
  Two caveats:
  - Short run: only about 4 of the 5 s samples fell under full load, because
    100K includes the warmup and ramp-up.
  - Snapshots only: RabbitMQ refreshes connection state every 5 s, so brief flow
    periods between snapshots can't be ruled out.

  The cause of the extra 1-server lag therefore stays unexplained. The remaining
  candidates are inside the broker's handling of one heavily loaded connection,
  or in server1 itself (34–48% CPU), and nothing measured so far separates
  them. I'll report it as an open question rather than a finding. Results are in
  results/flowcheck/flowcheck-1srv-100k/, including conn_state.csv.

  Restored to the originals (all verified)

  - consumer.env: identical to the original (cmp), with no tuning keys left. The
    consumer restarted on its defaults: threads=5 (min 1, max 10, autoscale 
    true), prefetch=100, ackBatch=1.
